| X-01 Spikes and ADRs 0014 to 0016 | ✅ Done: the four spikes, and ADRs 0014 to 0016 | Part 1, the clients: `a428dbc`<br>Part 1, Terraform: `c67448c`<br>Part 1, SAM: `1f6da7a`<br>Part 1, FF's front doors: `17e8c4f`<br>Part 2, versions: `516735b`<br>Part 3, keys: `d96bcac`<br>Part 4, SAM's YAML: `ab13da2`<br>The ADRs: `a2cb12d` | • **Part 1, what AWS's tools call, the three clients first** (`hack/spikes/lambda/`, its README has it all): a minimal Lambda endpoint, Node.js's standard library alone, D2's 41 operations in memory, a function `Pending` 3 s and an update `InProgress` 2 s as Lambda's are, every other operation refused saying it comes later, every request recorded; the AWS CLI 2.34.32, the SDK for JavaScript 3.1156.0 and boto3 1.43.118 each through a function's whole life (made, waited for, called with its log's tail and asynchronously, 6 MB of code, settings, a version, an alias called by name and by ARN, a URL, a permission, an SQS mapping, async settings, a concurrency, tags, every read, a later operation, a missing function, the deletes): every step went through, 168 requests, D2's operations and no other.<br>• **What the endpoint must do, learned:** every path signed with each segment escaped again, an ARN's `%3A` signed `%253A` (D3 holds, with SES's rule); the SDK for JavaScript signs `x-amz-content-sha256` and its own headers, boto3 and the CLI only `host`, `x-amz-date` and `content-type`, with no payload header, so the hash comes from the header when sent, from the body otherwise; the waiters poll `GetFunction`, boto3's every 1.0 s for up to 5 minutes, the SDK's from 1 s with jitter; a call while `Pending` or an update while `InProgress` is refused `409 ResourceConflictException`, which none of the three retries; the later refusal, `400 UnknownOperationException`, is shown with its message by each and retried by none; the largest body is the code, 6 MB of zip being 8.4 MB of JSON, sent with a `Content-Length`: Lambda's 50 MB zipped is about 67 MB, the body limit of the two code operations.<br>• **Part 1, Terraform:** the AWS provider 6.69.1 with OpenTofu 1.13.1 and with Terraform 1.16.6, from `hack/install-tools.sh`'s mirror: the reference modules' function resources (published, an alias, a public URL, a permission, an SQS mapping, async settings) applied, planned again with nothing to change, and destroyed, 49 requests each, once the endpoint answered as Lambda: **an alias with no other version's weight has no `RoutingConfig`, and async settings answer only what was set**, else the provider sees a change on every plan. It calls fewer operations than D2 thought (never the code-signing, tags or concurrency reads: `GetFunction` carries them), waits for `Active` with a `GetFunction` every 5 s, so a function made by Terraform costs 5 s at least, and adds two permissions of its own to a public URL, `FunctionURLAllowPublicAccess` and `FunctionURLAllowInvokeAction` (`InvokedViaFunctionUrl: true`, AWS's rule since 2025), which FF's `AddPermission` must take.<br>• **Part 1, SAM:** the SAM CLI 1.169.0 on a small app's template (a public URL, an SQS event, `AutoPublishAlias`, a queue, a bucket): `sam validate`, `sam validate --lint` and `sam build` **ask AWS nothing**, every endpoint pointed at the spike's; `--lint` refuses FF's region, `tn-tunis-1` not being in cfn-lint's list of AWS's, and passes with one of AWS's, which only picks the specification checked against; `sam build` leaves a folder a function, with its `node_modules`, and a template whose `CodeUri` names it: what `ff deploy` from a template (X-12) can take.<br>• **Part 1, FF's own front doors:** `front-doors.sh`, the provider and OpenTofu against FF's S3 front door and SQS endpoint on a stack of its own, with D10's provider block and a key of the account's, four flows applied, planned again and destroyed. **A queue and its dead-letter queue go through whole,** FF as it is: the provider passes over `ListQueueTags`' `UnsupportedOperation`, and waits for SQS's eventual consistency whatever FF answers, six matching reads 5 s apart after a queue is made (25 s), 15 not-founds 3 s apart after it is deleted (42 s). **A bucket can't be made or deleted:** `CreateBucket` and `DeleteBucket` answer `NotImplemented`, which step 2 left for this step and X-11 now lists, with the API's checks. **A bucket that exists goes through,** imported, with its website, a page and a notification: the provider reads it 15 times after each change and takes `NotImplemented` for "none" on all 12 reads FF doesn't offer, so their answers can wait. **Tags break it,** the provider's `default_tags` many teams set: a bucket's apply fails at `PutBucketTagging`, a queue is made again without its tags, and every plan after wants to add them; D10's tags are what make a plan clean. FF counts none of the refused requests. **The recordings kept:** `recordings/`, each request whole as signed, with the spike's own made-up key, for T-10's replays; the comments' task numbers made plain words.<br>• **Part 2, how a function's versions run** (`hack/spikes/versions/`, its README has it all): a function made through FF on a stack of its own, and beside its `$LATEST` a version 1 made by hand from `$LATEST`'s Deployment and Service, with a label `ff.dev/version` and `FUNCTION_VERSION` 1; 13 checks, about 2 minutes. **A version's first call from zero is a pod's start,** as `$LATEST`'s: ready after 4.0 to 5.2 s and answered 16 ms later, none of the three refused while its Service caught up; `$LATEST` from zero through FF 6.1 s. **`$LATEST`'s Service takes the version's pods:** it selects by the function's ID alone, and 43 of 100 calls to it were answered by version 1, while each Deployment kept its own pod; so each version's Service selects `ff.dev/version` too, `latest` for `$LATEST`, whose pods take the label in a rollout before its Service's selector does; the pods keep `ff.dev/function-id`, by which the log pipeline gives a line to its function. **FF counts one Deployment:** with two pods ready, `readyInstances` (the metrics, the idle sweep) and `readyInstancesByFunction` (the capacity) each said one; `maxInstances` caps a sum read by the function's label, and the scaler and the sweep must take every Deployment of the function. **An alias's share is the backend's choice at each call:** with a Service for each version, 107 of 1,000 calls went to version 1 at 10 % (76 to 124 is chance's 99 %), each answered by its version, none failed. **Read in the code:** a version copied from `$LATEST` would share one of its two environment Secrets, written in turn, and run `$LATEST`'s newer environment after its second change; a version gets a Secret of its own when it is published.<br>• **Part 3, keys that expire** (`hack/spikes/keys/`, its README has it all): **past its key's end, each SDK shows the service's own refusal at once,** the SDK for JavaScript 3.1156.0 and boto3 1.43.118 alike: one request each, `ExpiredToken` for S3 and SQS, `ExpiredTokenException` for SES and Lambda, the message as sent. **`AWS_CREDENTIAL_EXPIRATION` must not be given:** with it, boto3 sends nothing and raises `RuntimeError: Credentials were refreshed, but the refreshed credentials are still expired.`, from 10 minutes before the key's end; D5 gives none. **A rollout under load loses calls on production's path:** a function at one instance, four callers without pause, its environment changed (a new Secret, then the rollout, as a renewal will): through FF's API and the cluster API's proxy none of 573 and 587 calls failed, but straight to the Service, as the backend calls in production, 166 of 742 did: the backend's kept-alive connections went on reaching the old pod, which answers `503 Runtime is shutting down` while it drains, 162 times in 205 ms, and the 4 connections it closed as it went were reset. Every rollout of a busy function loses calls so today; X-08's new part 1 fixes it first. **The cost is small:** a rollout of one instance took 4 to 6 s and about 85 requests to the cluster's API; a hundred warm functions renewed every 6 hours make 17 rollouts, 17 Secrets written and about 1,400 requests an hour.<br>• **Part 4, SAM's YAML in Rust** (`hack/spikes/sam-yaml/`, its README has it all): a reader of about 300 lines on yaml-rust2 0.13's events, a crate of its own, gives a template as JSON with each short tag in its long form and each value's line and column by JSON pointer. **Five templates read value for value as cfn-flip 1.3.0, AWS's own converter, reads them:** `sam init`'s hello-world for Node.js and for Python (the SAM CLI 1.169.0), the small app, a fixture with all 18 short tags, and one of plain scalars. **CloudFormation reads YAML 1.1** ("CloudFormation supports the YAML Version 1.1 specification with a few exceptions", its guide), as cfn-flip and the SAM CLI do through PyYAML: the reader's first version, typing plain scalars as YAML 1.2, would have read 12 of the fixture's 17 values otherwise (`yes`, `0755`, `1:30`, `1e5` among them); it types them by 1.1's rules now. **What CloudFormation doesn't take is refused at its line:** aliases, merges, the `binary`, `omap`, `pairs`, `set` and `timestamp` tags, a tag it doesn't have, a key given twice, a second document. A tagged value's column is its text's, after the tag: yaml-rust2's events carry no tag's own position. The template's order is kept with serde_json's `preserve_order`, which in `ff` would reorder every map it prints: X-12 chooses.<br>• **The ADRs, from the plan's decisions and what the spikes measured:** [0014](adr/0014-lambda-api.md), Lambda's API (D1 to D8): the front door, the 41 operations and the refusal of the others, one signature check, keys at three levels, a function's key of 12 hours renewed with no call lost, once a rollout loses none (X-08's part 1), no `AWS_CREDENTIAL_EXPIRATION`; [0015](adr/0015-versions-and-aliases.md), versions and aliases (D9): each version frozen, its environment in a Secret of its own, in pods of its own made at its first call; a function's first publish moving `$LATEST` onto `ff.dev/version: latest`, its pods first and its Service's selector after, so that a function never published stays as today; the instances counted by the function's label; an alias chosen by the backend at each call; [0016](adr/0016-sam-templates.md), SAM templates (D11): read by `ff` as CloudFormation reads them, YAML 1.1 and its refusals at their line; the subset; a stack kept by FF with its resources, the parameters and the template's SHA-256, not its text. Three choices of the ADRs' own, beside the plan's: `$LATEST`'s label at the first publish rather than at the upgrade, YAML 1.1 rather than 1.2, and the stack without the template's text. |
| X-02 `ff export` | ✅ Done | Part 1, the code: `f677467`, the images' check: `1e09a4d`<br>Part 1, the endpoints: `263cfbe`<br>Part 1, the keys' uses: `63e977f`<br>Part 2, the route of a function's keys' uses: `0845823`<br>Part 2, the command, its template and runbook: `71b9dcd`<br>Part 2, the roles: `c4e9d7f`<br>Part 2, `terraform/`: `c898192`<br>Part 2, the tools' checks: `f5daf51`<br>Part 3, the guide: `1122d0a` | • **Part 1, the function's code as a zip AWS Lambda takes,** `GET /functions/:id/code`, with `functions:read`, so every member, viewers included: a package as it was uploaded, read from object storage; inline code as the one-file package its pods run, `function.ts` or `function.py`, byte for byte; with `javascript=true`, inline Node.js code as `function.js`, which `function.handler` names on Lambda as it named `function.ts` here, transpiled as FF's Node.js runtime transpiles it before running it (CommonJS, ES2022, `esModuleInterop`, enums and namespaces kept, no type checking, no source map), so that it runs on Lambda as it ran on FF; code with a syntax error, which never loaded on FF either, refused with its first three errors, each at its line and column, as the runtime words them. A package, and Python code, come as they are, `javascript` or not. The file is named after the function, any character Lambda's rule for names refuses, which a name from before the rule may hold, made `_`, so that the header holds it. Another account's function answers 404, as on every route; L5's two loops call the route under the rule every route with a function's ID takes. Next in part 1: `awsEndpoints`, then `key_uses`.<br>• **TypeScript in the backend's production image,** 24 MB, loaded the first time an export asks for JavaScript: the runtime's own transpiler, so that the JavaScript is what ran. Node's `stripTypeScriptTypes` would add nothing to the image, but it strips types alone and refuses an enum (`ERR_UNSUPPORTED_TYPESCRIPT_SYNTAX`), and its mode that transforms them is experimental and writes an ES module, which Lambda would load as `function.mjs` only, from code that ran on FF as CommonJS.<br>• **Refactored on the way:** the zip of one file taken out of `inlinePackage`, `oneFileZip`, which makes the JavaScript's zip too; a query parameter that is true or false, `booleanQuery`, which `DELETE /buckets/:name`'s `force` uses too, its refusal worded as before; the object store's two reads on one, so that a missing object is `StoredObjectMissingError` from both, where it was from `getFile` alone (a lost package is a platform failure either way, 500 with the request's ID, its log naming the object); the store's settings and client in five L2 specs, copies of each other, now `testObjectStore` and `testStoreClient`; the controller spec's Nest app, two copies, now `serve`, which the route's tests use too.<br>• **Tests:** L1 156 suites / 2,217 tests (was 154 suites / 2,196 tests): the transpile's JavaScript run as Node.js loads a CommonJS file (its types gone, an enum, a namespace, an import required, a default import as on FF; a type error passed over; a syntax error refused, three of four named), the zip of one file read back with yauzl as a package is, every byte, the object store's two reads on a stand-in store (whole with its type, to a file, missing, refused), the service's four cases and the per-account check, the route's headers, its `javascript` and an old name; L2 52 suites / 563 tests (was 51 / 561): a package of 4 MB kept in a real SeaweedFS and read back both ways, byte for byte, and a missing one said so by both; L5 1,040 checks (was 1,036), the route's four: 404 to another account's token and to its key, and to development mode's other account, and 401 without a token; `hack/ci.sh static` clean. The images' suite, on GitHub, then refused the backend's image, which it checked had no TypeScript: it checks now that TypeScript is there, and ts-node, Jest and the Nest CLI aren't.<br>• **Part 1, the endpoints a function takes, `awsEndpoints`:** `s3`, `sqs` and `sesv2`, as AWS's configuration names a service's endpoint; every one for every function, as before, and for a new one unless it names fewer, on create and on update, kept in that order. One left out loses its variable, `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_S3`, `_SQS` or `_SESV2`, at the rollout the change makes, so that the function can reach that service at AWS while FF still serves the others, one service at a time, as the parent plan's §7.2 moves; the same endpoints in another order change nothing. The function keeps FF's key and region, for the services FF serves: its code gives the client that goes to AWS an AWS key of its own, kept as a secret, and AWS's region, which the guide and the French page on buckets say how; the endpoints' variables stay FF's to set, as Lambda keeps the key's. The credentials' revision, which joins the environment's and rolls the pods out, stays the key's ID while a function takes every endpoint there is, so that no function rolls out for the change, and is the key's ID and the endpoints taken otherwise. A migration adds `aws_endpoints`, an array of an enum, every endpoint for every function. Part 1 ends with `key_uses`.<br>• **Tests:** L1 156 suites / 2,222 tests (was 156 / 2,217): the list on create and update, taken, refused (a service FF has no endpoint for, one named twice, not a list, null), the service's order kept, a change rolled out and recorded, the same endpoints in another order not; L2 52 suites / 565 tests (was 52 / 563): the key's variables with the endpoints taken, none too, its key and region still, each list a revision of its own, and a function taking every endpoint keeping the key's ID as its revision, where FF offers fewer too.<br>• **Part 1, what each key uses, `key_uses`:** the S3 front door, the SQS endpoint and the SES endpoint note, once a UTC day each, which bucket, queue or e-mail identity a key reached and by which of the service's operations, once its reach and its read-only check let it, and write them at the minute's turn, a row for each with its first and its last day, the first kept and the last moved on, never back, whichever backend's turn comes first; a turn that fails keeps its rows for the next, their days merged with any noted meanwhile, as the requests' counts are kept. **The operation, not read or write,** which the plan said: SAM's policy templates tell a queue's poller (receive, delete, change visibility) from its sender, and S3's reader from its writer, which read and write wouldn't, `DeleteMessage` being a write. A copy reads its source, `GetObject` on it, as IAM checks; an SES request names the identity it makes, reads or deletes, a message its sender's address, and the account's own requests (`ListBuckets`, `ListQueues`, `GetAccount`, the suppression list) name ''. **Its holder is the function for a function's key,** so that its uses stay together when X-08 replaces its key every few hours, and the key itself for the account's own. FF's API notes nothing: no key signs it, so its queues' calls, which share the SQS endpoint's operations, pass no `used` to them. A closed account's go with its data. The S3, SQS and SES services' enum, `AwsEndpoint`, moved to `common/aws-endpoint.ts`, as the keys' uses name the services too; the retention's comment on a closed account, ragged, reflowed; the retention spec's stand-in, which named each statement by a chain of patterns and any other "deliveries", names each by its table; the S3 front door spec's stand-in store deletes an object when told, where it answered 204 and kept it. Part 1 done: part 2, `ff export`, next.<br>• **Tests:** L1 157 suites / 2,229 tests (was 156 / 2,222): `KeyUses` on a stand-in repository (each use once a day, a function's two keys under the function, the next UTC day noted again, a failed turn kept with its first day, 500 rows a statement, what is left written when the backend stops), the S3 front door's notes (a bucket, a copy's source as `GetObject`, the account's list, a read-only key's refused write and a bucket out of reach not noted), the SQS operations' `used` (a queue reached, `ListQueues` the account's, nothing refused or missing), the SQS endpoint's with its key; L2 53 suites / 570 tests (was 52 / 565): the upsert on PostgreSQL, two backends' turns out of order, a sender of 320 characters; S3's rows after the front door's suite, every operation it ran, not another account's bucket; SQS's from the endpoint and none from FF's API; SES's, the identity made, read and deleted, a sender, the account's own, and a read-only key's refused change absent; a closed account's uses deleted with its data.<br>• **Part 2 begins with the route the export reads its roles from, `GET /functions/:id/key-uses`,** with `functions:read`, every member: what the function's keys used, by service, in the services' order, resource and operation, each with its first and its last day, the function's own whatever key it held; another account's function 404, as on every route. **Tests:** L1 157 suites / 2,232 tests (was 157 / 2,229): the route's answer read under the function, a function not the caller's 404 with no uses read, its rule beside the other reads; L2 53 suites / 571 tests (was 53 / 570): a holder's uses in order, not another holder's nor another account's; L5 1,044 checks (was 1,040), the route's four: 404 to another account's token, to its key and to development mode's other account, and 401 without a token.<br>• **Part 2, the command, `ff export <folder> [--functions a,b] [--with-values]`** (`cli/src/commands/export/`): it reads the account through FF's API, a function at a time (its settings and variables, its secrets' names, its URL and the URL's domains, its schedules, the queues it reads, its settings for calls that don't wait), then the queues, the buckets with their notifications and sites, the databases, caches and mail; with `--functions`, those functions alone, the buckets that call them and the queues they read or send to, these queues' dead-letter queues too, a name the account lacks refused before anything is written. In a folder that must be new or empty, it writes: **`template.yaml`** for AWS SAM, from a YAML writer of its own (each map in its order, a string plain only where YAML 1.1 reads it back as itself, so that `yes`, `0755` or `2010-09-09` are quoted, CloudFormation's short tags), each function an `AWS::Serverless::Function` (`nodejs24.x` or `python3.13`, FF's being Node.js 24 and Python 3.13; its memory and timeout; its variables as parameters unless `--with-values`, its secrets as `NoEcho` ones; `minInstances` as provisioned concurrency on an alias `live`; a `maxInstances` other than FF's 3 as reserved concurrency; its URL with its CORS, the address in `Outputs`; its schedules as `Schedule` events, an enabled one on at AWS when `ScheduleState` says `ENABLED` and off until then, so that no run happens on both; the queues it reads as `SQS` events; the buckets' events to it as `S3` events; its settings for calls that don't wait as `EventInvokeConfig`, a function destination by its ARN, so that two functions naming each other make no cycle), each queue with its attributes and dead-letter queue, its visibility timeout raised to its readers' longest timeout where shorter, which AWS refuses; each bucket, its name a parameter defaulting to FF's, S3's names being everyone's, its events to queues with the policy that lets S3 send them; **`template-before-copy.yaml`**, the same without the buckets' events, for the first deploy, so that copying the files calls nothing; **`code/<function>.zip`**, from `GET /functions/:id/code?javascript=true`; **`secrets.template.env`**, each value the template takes and the export doesn't write, a line each with what it is; **`data/`**, the pages for what the account has, the account's names under each title, `s3-sync.sh` executable, and the new **`lambda.md`**: a function's settings as Lambda's, the deploy with `sam deploy --guided`, and a custom domain in front of a function URL through CloudFront, with its template; **`MIGRATION.md`**, the parent plan's runbook in the order that works, each step with the account's own resources and commands: before the move (the account, Lambda's quotas with the instances counted, the values, a staging account), the stack deployed and called by nobody, the data (the databases, the buckets copied then their events added, the sites, the caches, the mail), the queues, the functions (each one's callers, its domains, its schedules disabled on FF before `ScheduleState=ENABLED`), then FF left; and what the template changes on the way: a name from before Lambda's rule, FF's original event, an `AWS_IAM` URL, which takes IAM's signatures and no API key, a custom domain, warm instances billed, a cap taken from the quota, a visibility timeout raised, a destination outside the export, a site. **A site's bucket goes to `data/s3-website.md`'s template**, CloudFront in front of a private bucket, as FF serves a site over HTTPS, rather than to `template.yaml`, where S3's website configuration would serve it over HTTP from a public bucket; D12 named sites in the template. Next: the roles from `key_uses`, `terraform/`, then the tools' checks on every export. **Refactored on the way:** the client reads an answer as bytes (`bytes`), and an answer that may be absent as none (`json_if_any`), of which `ff url` and `ff async-config` had copies; the name in an ARN, three copies, one helper (`name_in_arn`), which reads a function's name without its version too; `ff init`'s check of a new folder shared (`new_folder`); the records the export reads, the other commands' own, opened to their module rather than copied; the scripted API answers bytes. **Tests:** the CLI's 314 in 22 binaries (was 301 in 21): the YAML writer (YAML 1.1's words and numbers quoted, colons, tags, comments, line breaks, nesting); logical IDs and Lambda's names (a name's words, a second of the same, a name from before the rule); an account of three functions, three queues, a bucket with events and a site, a database, a cache and a mail domain, its template whole (`testdata/shop-template.yaml`, which `sam validate --lint` takes, the SAM CLI 1.169.0 with cfn-lint), the notes, the first deploy's template, `--with-values`, what `--functions` keeps, the kit's files and pages, the values to fill, the runbook's order and names; and the binary against the scripted API, every route it reads in order, the zip as served, the folder's files, a folder with files refused before any call, a function the account lacks refused with nothing written; the `ff` suite's 223 checks as before, the commands refactored on the way (`ff url`, `ff async-config`, `ff event-sources`, `ff buckets notifications`, `ff init`) against a real backend; `hack/ci.sh static` clean.<br>• **Part 2, each function's role, from what its keys used** (`cli/src/commands/export/roles.rs`): the export reads `GET /functions/:id/key-uses` after a function's other settings, and gives the function `Policies` allowing what its keys used of the account's buckets, queues and mail on FF. Each operation becomes the IAM action AWS checks for it, as S3's, SQS's and SES's documentation says: a head as a read, a copy and each part of an upload as `s3:PutObject`, a copy's source as the read FF noted, a listing or `HeadBucket` as `s3:ListBucket`, a batch as its single operation, `ListBuckets` as `s3:ListAllMyBuckets`. **SAM's policy templates where the function did what one is for,** each on its one bucket or queue: `S3CrudPolicy` for a bucket it read, wrote and deleted in, else `S3ReadPolicy` for a read and `S3WritePolicy` for a write; `SQSSendMessagePolicy` for a send, `SQSPollerPolicy` for a receive, so that a queue's sender can't read it nor a bucket's reader write it, which read and write wouldn't tell; then one policy of statements for the rest: by bucket, by its objects, by queue, by identity, and the account's own on `*`. A message is allowed on the identity it is sent as, as FF sends it: its address when the account proved it, else its domain. A bucket is named by its name's parameter, so that a bucket calling a function its role names makes no cycle; a queue by its attributes; a site's bucket, which `data/s3-website.md`'s stack makes, by a parameter of its own, FF's name until its stack's output is given; a bucket or a queue the account no longer has by its name. With `--functions`, the export keeps the buckets and the queues the functions' keys used too. `MIGRATION.md` says the days the uses were seen, and that one FF didn't see then, such as a monthly job's, AWS refuses with `AccessDenied` until the function's `Policies` allow it; a function whose keys used nothing, a site's bucket, a bucket or a queue gone, an operation the export knows no action of, are notes; `data/lambda.md` says how roles are made. **Refactored on the way:** a queue's ARN, its attribute or in the stack's account and region, three copies (a queue read, a dead-letter queue, a destination), one helper; the roles' tests and the account's fixture one helper for a function's uses. **Tests:** the CLI's 319 in 22 binaries (was 314 in 22): the roles alone (a bucket's templates and its other actions by scope, a queue's sender and reader told apart and a batch as its single operation, mail sent as the address or its domain, the account's own on `*`, an operation the export knows no action of, the days seen); the template whole again (`testdata/shop-template.yaml`, which `sam validate --lint` takes, the SAM CLI 1.169.0 with cfn-lint), every kind of grant in it; the notes; what `--functions` keeps; the runbook's days; the binary reading `/functions/f1/key-uses` in its turn; `hack/ci.sh static` clean.<br>• **Part 2, `terraform/`, the same for OpenTofu or Terraform with AWS's provider** (`cli/src/commands/export/terraform.rs`, from an HCL writer of the export's own, `hcl.rs`, which writes as `tofu fmt` leaves a file: two spaces a level, the `=` of each run of one-line attributes aligned, a value over several lines or a block ending the run, a key HCL wouldn't take bare quoted, a string's `${` and `%{` doubled so that it is never a template, a template's kept, calls such as `jsonencode({ … })` written over their lines): `main.tf`, the tool (`>= 1.6`) and the provider (`~> 6.0`), the account's ID and the document that lets Lambda take a role; `variables.tf`, the region, `schedule_state` (`DISABLED` until the move) and `bucket_events` (`false` until the files are copied), so that one apply after another moves as the template's two deploys do, each bucket's name and each value of the functions', a secret `sensitive`; `functions.tf`, each function's role (`name_prefix`, the log's managed policy, and a policy of statements, action by action, Terraform having no policy templates: what its keys used, and what SAM would add itself, the queues it reads for Lambda's poller and where its calls' records go), the function, its alias `live` with provisioned concurrency for warm instances, its URL with its CORS and, open to anyone, the permission it needs, its schedules as EventBridge rules with their targets and permissions, the queues it reads, its settings for calls that don't wait; `queues.tf`, the queues, a visibility timeout raised as in the template, and the policy that lets S3 send a bucket's events; `buckets.tf`, the buckets, the permissions that let them call the functions, through the alias where there is one, and their notifications, made once `bucket_events` is true; `outputs.tf`, the URLs; and `terraform.tfvars.example`, each value to fill, with the region, a line each with where it comes from. A file with nothing to hold isn't written. Names are the FF names' words in small letters joined by `_`, each once for its type. `MIGRATION.md` and `data/lambda.md` say how to use it instead of the template, not beside it. **Refactored on the way:** the queues' raised visibility timeouts, which both files need, computed by the account (`raised_visibility`), the template keeping its notes; the hint of each value to fill one helper for both files; the roles' statements alone, for Terraform, one function with the template's (`role_with`); an address in an SES identity's ARN escaped for `!Sub`, which an address may hold `${` for. **Tests:** the CLI's 327 in 22 binaries (was 319): the writer (alignment as `tofu fmt` does it, lists of names and of objects, a string's escapes, a template, a call, JSON as HCL), Terraform's names, the roles in statements alone; the shop account's six files whole (`testdata/shop-terraform/`, which `tofu fmt -check` and `tofu validate` take, OpenTofu 1.13.1 with AWS's provider 6.71.0), values written with `--with-values`, the files an account without queues, buckets or URLs has; the kit's files and its values to fill; the runbook's Terraform path; the binary's `terraform/` files; `hack/ci.sh static` clean.<br>• **Part 2, the tools' checks on an export the suites make:** the ff suite's new section, `small_app_exported`, minutes after the small app ran (the sections that wait on the clock between), so that its keys' uses are written by then, and waited for when it runs alone: `ff export` of the account, the kit's files there; shop-receipts's role writing the receipts (`S3WritePolicy`) and sending from the proved address alone (`ses:SendEmail` on its identity), as its keys did, and neither reading the bucket nor sending to the queue; shop-api's sending to the queue of new orders (`SQSSendMessagePolicy`), which it can't read, and reading the receipts (`S3ReadPolicy`); the template taken by `sam validate --lint`, cfn-lint inside it; `terraform/` left as it is by `tofu fmt -check`, and taken by `tofu init` and `tofu validate`, AWS's provider from hack/install-tools.sh's mirror; then the small app removed, as its own section did. L4 makes no export: it drives the API, not ff. **On GitHub:** the ff suite's stage installs the SAM CLI, OpenTofu and AWS's provider as hack/install-tools.sh pins them (`deps`), and its job keeps them between runs, 480 MB, keyed by that script. **Tests:** the ff suite's 228 checks (was 223), the new section's five, which took 100 s run alone with what it needs (77 checks), its wait for the minute's turn among them, and about 75 s without it, 120 its budget; `hack/ci.sh static` clean. Part 2 done.<br>• **Part 3, the guide:** a section of its own, [Moving to AWS](getting-started.md#moving-to-aws-ff-export), after the small app's walkthrough: the command's three forms, what the folder holds, a row each, how each function's role is made and what a use FF never saw does, and the checks before a deploy; the walkthrough exports the small app before it is deleted, its roles said. Exit 1 on an error, and with `--strict` on a warning (a new kind, `Findings`, in ADR 0002's table and the CLI's README); `--output json`, the findings and the counts. On the repository's own code: the examples give 2 warnings, each right (`hello.ts`, `FF_WEBHOOK_SECRET` in a receiver that isn't a function), FF's Python runtime 7, its own variables and `/proc`. Not seen: a variable read through an alias (`const env = process.env`), a destructuring over several lines. The CLI: 352 tests in 23 binaries, 336 in 22 before.<br>• **Part 1, the template:** X-01's YAML reader brought into `ff` as `cli/src/sam.rs`, its tests with it, yaml-rust2 0.13 without its decoder of other encodings (ADR 0016's dependency, now `ff`'s); serde_json's `preserve_order` left off, as ADR 0016 asks, a value's line giving the template's order where it matters. A folder's `template.yaml` or `template.yml`, or that file alone: `template`, what CloudFormation can't read, at its line and column (an alias, a merge, a tag it hasn't, a key twice), an error; `lambda-limits`, `MemorySize` or `Timeout` of a function (SAM's or Lambda's own) or of `Globals.Function` outside Lambda's 128 to 10,240 MB and 1 to 900 s, whole numbers, where it is written, a value from an intrinsic left to the deploy, an error; `typescript-handler`, a Node.js function's handler that is TypeScript alone in its `CodeUri` folder, unless its `Metadata: BuildMethod` is esbuild or its code a zip, a warning, the folder's own handler then not looked for. `sam build`'s `.aws-sam/` is neither read nor counted in the size. `ff export`'s template and the spike's fixtures, all 18 tags among them, read clean. **A template outside the subset is X-12's:** the export's templates hold more than D11 lists (`FunctionName`, `ProvisionedConcurrencyConfig`, `AWS::SQS::QueuePolicy`), so the subset is settled with `ff deploy`'s reading, which `ff doctor` will share. The CLI: 363 tests in 23 binaries<br>• **Part 2, the account:** `ff doctor --deployed [--strict]` reads the account as `ff export` does (its reader, the same requests, reads alone) and names, a line each as `resource: warning: message [check]`, what a move changes that the code doesn't show: `lambda-name`, a function whose name is outside Lambda's rule, with the name the export gives it; `wrapped-event`, one that gets FF's original event; `warm-instances`, one kept warm, which AWS bills as provisioned concurrency; `url-keys`, a URL whose auth is `AWS_IAM`, which takes FF's API keys, where AWS asks for a request signed with its own (SigV4); `custom-domain`, a domain on a function's URL or a bucket's site, which moves behind CloudFront; `ff-endpoints`, a function whose key reached FF's S3, SQS or SES, by service with what its key's daily uses name, and the last day; `storage-key`, each of the account's storage keys with what it reaches and when it was used last, or how to revoke one never used. Warnings each, which `--strict` fails on (exit 1). A developer's token can't list the storage keys: that is said on stderr and in the JSON's `notChecked`, and the rest is checked. **`--deployed`, not `--account`,** which is `ff`'s global flag. **FF's API keys aren't listed:** only an owner's login lists them, never a key. **Refactors:** the export's notes on FF's original event and on a renamed function are a constant and a function the doctor shares, the export's notes unchanged; `ff buckets keys list`'s reading of the keys and what each reaches are functions the doctor calls; the export's fixture account shared with the doctor's tests; a test that clap takes every command as defined, which caught `--account`'s clash. The CLI: 368 tests in 23 binaries, 363 before; the `ff` suite (`hack/cli-e2e.sh`) 235 checks, 231 before: the small app's folder clean, then `--deployed` naming shop-receipts' S3 and SES and shop-api's SQS, `--strict` failing with 1, and the same findings after the export<br>• **Part 3, the guide:** the getting-started guide's "Check the code first" and "What still points at FF", and the French guide's, written with parts 1 and 2; `MIGRATION.md`, the runbook `ff export` writes, gains `ff doctor` on each function's folder before the move and `ff doctor --deployed` before deleting on FF. **The CLI plan's C-33 marked done,** each check it listed said where it is. A version runs in a Deployment and a Service of its own, `fn-<id>-v<n>`, its pods labelled `ff.dev/version` with its number, which both select, `FUNCTION_VERSION` set to it, and its environment in a Secret of its own, `fn-<id>-v<n>-env`, as it was published, never one of `$LATEST`'s two. They are made at the version's first call, with one instance, from the workload the call gives, and deleted with the version, or with its function, before its record: a removal that fails keeps the version, to be deleted again. As ADR 0015 says, each new version's publish starts moving `$LATEST`'s pods onto `ff.dev/version: latest`, in its Deployment's template, which rolls them, without waiting for the rollout, so that a version's first call seldom waits for it; that call finishes the move, the label put in `$LATEST`'s Service's selector once all its pods carry it, so that the Service never takes a version's pod for its own nor is left without a pod. A failure to start the move at a publish is only logged, as the first call makes it anyway. The account's quota allows a Service and a Secret more for each pod it allows, for the versions'. FF's count of a function's ready instances adds up its Deployments, its versions' included; the check of the images pods mount reads `$LATEST`'s alone, as the restore rolls `$LATEST` out. Nothing calls a version yet: the next part's `qualifier` does.<br>• **Found by L3:** an update of `$LATEST` took the label back out of its Service's selector, so that `$LATEST`'s calls could reach a version's pods again: the API server takes a Service's selector as one field, which an apply replaces whole, whoever set its keys. SQS and SES share their words (`serviceWords`: IncompleteSignature for what is missing or malformed, SignatureDoesNotMatch for the rest, each service's own error for a token), and their readers are a few lines each. S3's reader, renamed `s3-signature.ts` from `sigv4.ts` beside `sqs-signature.ts` and `ses-signature.ts`, keeps its presigned URLs, `x-amz-content-sha256` and its own words on top, and `aws-chunked.ts` its streaming chunks; `signedForStore` builds its canonical request with the check's own. For every service but S3 the body's hash is the body's own, never `x-amz-content-sha256`'s on its word: in X-01's recordings the SDK for JavaScript sends the body's hash there, in each of the 57 requests kept with their body, and the other tools don't send it. ADR 0014 says it as built.<br>• **AWS's published suite** in L1: `aws-c-auth`'s 38 cases at commit `3d29adc`, in `BE/src/common/signature-v4-suite/cases.json` with its Apache 2.0 licence and notice; each case's canonical request, string to sign and signature, signed in a header and presigned in a query, the path in S3's form or every other service's as the case says; each header-signed request read and checked whole, passed with the key's secret, refused with another or changed; the cases with a session token refused, as FF never issues one. | X-08 Functions' keys that expire | ✅ Done | Part 1: `28660b0`<br>Before part 2, one lock for the jobs that run in one backend at a time: `6e87542`<br>Before part 2, `$LATEST`'s rollouts with its last version, a bug: `917f826`<br>Part 2: `b87ca55`<br>Part 3: `2d8551a`<br>Part 2's L2 test of a call's usage, fixed: `c7427ed`<br>L3's stand-in for the functions' table, fixed: `c8f9a67`<br>A pod's last calls, after GitHub's run of 2026-11-02: `661807f` | • **Part 1, no call lost while a function's pods are replaced,** taken before phases 1 and 2, as a production bug: X-01's spike found that every rollout of a busy function lost calls on production's path, a deploy's and a scale-down's as much as a renewal's. **What was lost, and why:** a runtime told to stop answered `503 Runtime is shutting down` to every call the backend's kept-alive connections still brought it, 162 times in 205 ms, and reset the 4 connections it closed as it went. Two more ways, read in the code: the calls waiting their turn behind a slow call were cut at the drain's deadline, which the spike's 50 ms calls couldn't show; and the Python runtime, its accept loop stopped, left new connections unanswered in its listener's queue until it exited.<br>• **The runtimes, both:** told to stop, a runtime answers the call it runs and refuses every other, one waiting its turn or one that comes on a connection still open, with `503` and `X-FF-Not-Run: shutting-down`, the call never run. Each answer closes its connection (`Connection: close`), so that the caller's next call opens one the Service gives a ready pod. It closes no other itself, since a call could be crossing it: it waits for its callers to close them, the backend each one it leaves unused for 5 s, and exits once nothing runs and no connection is open, at the latest a second past the function's timeout or those 5 s, within the pod's grace period (the timeout and 5 s). The Node.js runtime stops accepting with `net.Server`'s close, since `http.Server`'s closes the idle connections too; the Python runtime closes its listener as its accept loop stops, and its calls take turns on a condition, which the stop wakes where a lock couldn't be. Both READMEs gain a section, Stopping.<br>• **The backend:** a call refused as never run is tried again, on a connection of its own, since one kept alive could lead back to the pod that refused, as a connection refused is, for 5 s from the first refusal rather than from the call's start, so that a call refused after waiting its turn still has them; in both modes. Direct calls keep their connections in a pool of their own, each closed after 5 s unused, which is what the runtimes wait for; the proxied path moved into a method of its own. A reset on a kept-alive connection is not tried again: the call may have run, when its pod died under it, and HTTP says a POST that may have been applied is not sent again on the client's own; ADR 0014 lists it and the usual `preStop` sleep among the alternatives.<br>• **Measured, the keys spike run again** (`load.mjs` on the backend's new rule, the runtime's image built from the fix; its cleanup, which took the load's ConfigMap for a pod's name, fixed): none of 569 calls lost through FF's API, of 578 through the cluster API's proxy, and of 576 straight to the Service, where 166 of 742 were lost before; directly, 3 calls refused as never run and 5 whose new connection was refused were answered when tried again, the slowest in 1.8 s. The old pod lives longer in development: gone 0.3 s after the change answered through the proxy, but 6.1 and 7.5 s on the other paths, where the cluster API's proxy still held idle connections to it, which it keeps 90 s, so the runtime waited until its limit. A rollout of one instance: 7.4 s, 95 requests to the cluster's API.<br>• **Tests:** the Node.js runtime's L1 60 (was 57: the call waiting refused at once, the idle connection left to its caller, an idle runtime's exit at once), its probes now each on a connection of their own, since fetch keeps its connections as long as the runtime's Keep-Alive says; the Python runtime's 35 (was 32, the same three); the orchestrator's spec 85 (was 81: tried again on a connection of its own, the few seconds counted from the first refusal, given up with the last refusal, a proxied call tried again but no other 503), its two retry tests moved into a group of their own on shared helpers, where three tests had a copy each of the stand-in pod and the redirect; the backend's L1 154 suites / 2,196 tests (was 154 / 2,192), `tsc` and ESLint clean. Run on the code before, in a worktree: the runtimes' first two new tests fail, as do the orchestrator's four; each runtime's third passes there too, a guard that the new wait leaves an idle runtime's exit at once.<br>• **Corrected to what was built:** ADR 0014 §5, its consequences and its alternatives; the plan's X-08 and its risk row; the tasks list. **Left for part 2:** the L3 case of a rollout under load, with the renewal it is for.<br>• **Before part 2, one lock for the jobs that run in one backend at a time** (2026-11-02, a refactor of its own): the hourly rating, the caches', databases' and buckets' samples and the restore of lost code images each took PostgreSQL's advisory lock with a copy of the same dozen lines; they share `withJobLock` (`BE/src/database/job-lock.ts`), which part 2's renewal of functions' keys takes too, its statements the same, so that the restore's test of them passes unchanged. L1 **167 suites / 2,518 tests** (166 / 2,515 before): the helper's three, its turn, another's and a failure giving the lock back; L2's rating and samples: 4 suites / 55 tests, all passed, as many.<br>• **Before part 2, `$LATEST`'s rollouts with its last version's number** (2026-11-02, a bug found while reading what the renewal will roll out): a function's record isn't read with `lastVersion`, and the backend built `$LATEST`'s workload from the record alone, so that each create, update, rollback and restore of a function with versions applied `$LATEST`'s template without its label and its Service with the function's ID alone, where ADR 0015 keeps both on `latest`: its Service then took a version's running pods for its own, and `$LATEST`'s calls could run a version's code, until that version's next first call moved them back. The workload now reads the number when the record hasn't it, one query a rollout. L2's versions **23 tests** with deploy keys' (22 before): `$LATEST` rolled out with 0, 1 and 2 as versions are published; L1 for functions, storage, mail, queues and URLs 64 suites / 1,004 tests, all passed.<br>• **Part 2, keys that end** (2026-11-02). **A key past its end is refused as expired** at each front door, in its service's words, where it was refused as one FF doesn't know: S3's `400 ExpiredToken`, SQS's `ExpiredToken` (`ExpiredTokenException` in its JSON shape), SES's `ExpiredTokenException`, a URL's `403 Forbidden: The security token included in the request is expired.`; a deploy's key, an hour long, ends so too. **A function's key ends 12 hours after it is made;** past half its life, the function's next workload renews it, the old key marked `replaced_at` (migration `FunctionKeyRenewal`: the index of a function's working key counts the keys neither revoked nor replaced) and working on beside the new one, a second renewal at the same moment taking the first's key. **The renewal job** (`FunctionKeyRenewal`), every `FF_KEY_RENEWAL_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (60) under the jobs' lock: of the functions due or with keys replaced, each with instances asked has each Deployment, `$LATEST`'s and its versions', rolled onto the current key in turn (`renewEnvironment`: the Secret, then the template alone, its pods replaced one at a time), waited for; a Deployment whose template differs by more than its key is left to the change that wrote it; the turn that finds every Deployment on the current key revokes the replaced ones. A call from zero brings a fresh key when its key is due. A function's key from before has no end, and works on (part 3). **Found and fixed with it:** a version's wait for its rollout would have deleted `$LATEST`'s environment Secrets, the wait deleting those its Deployment doesn't read; only `$LATEST`'s wait does now. **Refactored with it:** the Secret-then-Deployment write is one method (`applyTemplate`), shared by an apply, the renewal and a cold start; a version's Secret one (`applyVersionEnvironment`), shared with its deploy; the code image a template mounts one helper (`codeImageOf`), shared with the list of code images; a published version's lookup one (`publishedVersion`), shared by calls and the renewal; L4's three readers of the AWS CLI's refusals one (`outcome`), every service's code read alike. **L4 moves keys' times in SQL** rather than giving the stack a short life, as the tasks said, so that it runs the 12 hours shipped, the renewal's pace alone shortened (5 s); its key is the function's own, copied out of its pod, since an account's key never ends. **Fixed in the suites, from df44343's run on GitHub:** L4's account made more than the beta plan's 60 changes a minute once the URL section's three keys came, so that the buckets section's tenth key got 429 and the keys left behind failed ten checks after it: L4's own database gives the beta plan 10,000, the rate limit checked on the small plan's five as before; L5's audit log gains the URL case's key, made and revoked. **Tests:** L1 **167 suites / 2,527 tests** (167 / 2,518 before): each front door's refusal, the renewal in two writes, the cold start with a fresh key, the setting; L2's keys, renewal, mail, versions and deploy keys **8 suites / 69 tests**, the job's 5 and the key's life 5 new, migration:check with 72; L3's orchestrator **17 passed and 2 skipped** (16 and 2): a function at two instances called without pause by four callers through its renewal's rollout, none lost, and one at zero starting on a fresh key; L4's new section `key_life` **11 checks** in 32 s, and the sections the helper touched with it, 198, all passed; L5 **1,116 checks**, all passed (1,115 and the audit log's failure on GitHub); `tsc` and ESLint clean. **Corrected:** ADR 0014's as-built notes (part 2, and part 3 of X-07, whose URL now says a key expired); the guide on a function's key; `BE/.env.example`; the deploy key's description, and a test's name that still counted the plan's two keys.<br>• **Part 3, the keys of before** (2026-11-02). A function's key with no end, made before keys ended, is due at once, as one past half its life is: the function's next workload replaces it, and the renewal lists it as lasting. Its first turn after the upgrade renews it for every function, one scaled to zero too, its templates written with the new key and no pod started, since a lasting key never ends on its own; a function with no Deployment has it revoked at once (`revokeLasting`), its next rollout making it a key; the turn after revokes the keys replaced. So within two turns of the upgrade no function holds a key that lasts, and a key copied out of a function stops working within 12 hours, which the guide now says. **The cost,** X-01's measure, is in ADR 0014's notes: 17 rollouts and about 1,400 requests to the cluster's API an hour for a hundred warm functions; the upgrade's first turn rolls each running function once, one after another, about 8,500 requests over a quarter of an hour for a hundred; a turn with nothing due asks the cluster nothing. **The platform's suite carries a function across a renewal:** the function that writes to its bucket with its own key, in gVisor, its key aged six hours in SQL, the renewal every 5 s in the suite's overlay (`FF_KEY_RENEWAL_INTERVAL_SECONDS`), called each second until the old key is revoked, every call answered; its run on GitHub is in the next entry. **Tests:** L1 **167 suites / 2,527 tests**, as many, part 3's code having no L1 test of its own; L2's keys, renewal, mail, versions and deploy keys **8 suites / 72 tests** (69): a lasting key due at once, listed and replaced; revoked alone when asked; renewed by the job at zero with no pod started and revoked the turn after; that of a function with no Deployment revoked at once; L4's `key_life` **13 checks** in 49 s (11): a key from before, the function at zero, replaced and revoked, its next call on the new key; `tsc` and ESLint clean.<br>• **Part 2's L2 test of a call's usage, fixed** (2026-11-02): part 2's run on GitHub (41413165966) failed L2's `usage.db.spec.ts`, which expected `$LATEST`'s call to give the cluster no way to start it, where it now gives a fresh key when its key is due; the L2 runs before had taken the keys' suites alone. It expects the start now, and the full L2 ran here: **56 suites / 634 tests**, all passed, migration:check with 72.<br>• **On GitHub, and L3's stand-in fixed** (2026-11-02): run 41415711258, on `c7427ed` with the platform's suite, passed it, the function carried across its key's renewal in gVisor with every call answered, and static, L1, L2, L4, L5 and the CLI's suites. It failed L3: the stand-in for the functions' table that L3's lifecycle and scaling suites use had no `findOne` outside a transaction, which the workload reads `$LATEST`'s last version's number with since `917f826`, so each deploy there failed, 7 tests; the L3 runs before had taken the orchestrator's suite alone. The stand-in's manager is one object now, the same for a lookup and inside a transaction, and its two comments lose their task numbers. **L3 here** (`hack/k8s/test-it.sh`): **4 suites, 33 tests passed and 2 skipped**, as before `917f826`.<br>• **A pod's last calls, after GitHub's run of 2026-11-02** (2026-11-02): run 41422051721, on `79d0f4e` with the platform's suite, passed static, L1, L2, L3, L5 and the CLI's suites, and failed two checks that had passed before. **L4's was its own:** its keys section scaled the function to zero by hand right after a minute of calls, and the backend's autoscaler, which steps a function called in the last minute back up from zero, started a pod again (its log: from 0 to 1 instances), so the key from before keys ended was replaced with a pod running (`t t 1 true 12`, where 0 was meant). Its two functions are at zero now before any call, the key from before keys ended the second one's. **The platform's was a call lost at the renewal,** the first in its runs: the loop took 50 s where it takes 12, one call hanging about 42 s, the runtime's wait for a free instance, the timeout and 2 s, on a connection open with no answer; most likely made to the stopping pod after its runtime had closed its listener, the node's rules not caught up yet. A pod now leaves its Service 5 seconds before its runtime is told to stop (`STOP_DELAY_SECONDS`, a `preStop` sleep, which needs no shell in the image), its grace period those 5 seconds longer; a function has it at its next rollout. **And one found reading the job:** the old key was revoked once every Deployment's template was on the current key, while a pod told to stop could still be answering its call with the old one; the turn lists the function's pods now, and waits while one runs with another environment (`Orchestrator.podRevisions()`). Both suites name each lost call with its times and its answer, and the platform's keeps the backend's log from before the redeploy for customers' domains, which took it with the old pod. **Tested here:** L1 **169 suites / 2561 tests** (the template's delay and grace period; the pods' revisions read, one told to stop counted, one ended not); L2's keys **2 suites / 22 tests** (the old key kept while a pod told to stop runs with it, revoked once it has ended), migration:check with 72; L3 (`hack/k8s/test-it.sh`) **4 suites, 33 tests passed and 2 skipped**, on a second run: the first failed one wait for a namespace's deletion, the machine having slept 75 minutes in it; L4 **469 checks passed**, its keys section 14, in 69 s, its budget 80 s now, where it was 60; static passed. And a client gone before its body ended was meant to be refused as incomplete, in each server's words, but Node.js reports it as an `aborted` error before the close, so it reached each server as a failure of its own, logged as unexpected: the reader now refuses it as incomplete, which its new test found. **Tests:** L1 **168 suites / 2,532 tests** (167 / 2,527 before): the reader's five, a body whole, one declared too large, one sent too large to a client still sending, a client gone, and the two small helpers; the front doors' and the gateway's own tests pass unchanged; `tsc` and ESLint clean.<br>• **Part 1, the front door** (2026-11-02; functions reach it from their pods in the next commit): a server of the backend's own (`BE/src/lambda/`) on `FF_LAMBDA_PORT` (3006) at `FF_LAMBDA_BASE`, which production requires on `https://`. It takes the operation by its method and dated path among the 41, reads the body up to Lambda's own limit for it, checks the signature for `lambda` through X-07's module, and the key: none, unknown, past its end or wrong, each refused in Lambda's words; then the account, active; then what the request names, a function by its name, its ARN or its partial ARN, its qualifier from the name or the query; the key's level with it (read, invoke, write, as IAM groups Lambda's actions; a function's own key invokes alone), each refusal saying what the key may do; another account's or region's function answered as a missing one, `404 ResourceNotFoundException`; a change counted against the plan's changes a minute in the API's own count, `429 TooManyRequestsException` past it; the audit log's actor the key. Each answer has `x-amzn-RequestId`, each refusal Lambda's shape and `x-amzn-ErrorType`. No operation is built yet: each answers `400 UnknownOperationException`, saying FF doesn't offer it yet. **The platform:** the Service's port `lambda`, reached by the edge alone; the edge's `FF_LAMBDA_HOST`, which `deploy.sh` checks against `FF_LAMBDA_BASE`; the development server's eighth fixed name, `lambda.dev.ffunc.cloud`, whose record at OVH must exist before the next `install.sh`, its certificate waited for and its unsigned refusal checked by `server.sh`; every suite's backend has it on a free port, or none (`FF_LAMBDA_PORT=0`), so that none takes 3006. **Refactored with it:** a request's path and query as sent, one helper (`requestTarget`) where the SES, SQS and S3 front doors and the URL gateway each split the target their own way; Its worker's streams are pipes now, which the main process reads, each on a thread of its own, and forwards as they come (`ff_runtime/output.py`), as the Node.js runtime's main thread forwards its worker's: the worker ends each call's output on both with an end-of-call marker, which the main process takes out, writing `END` once both have come, a second at most, or once the streams of a worker stopped at a timeout have ended, so that what the call wrote comes ahead of the timeout's line; its own lines start a line of their own after one the call left open, as the Node.js runtime's do. `Tracing` and `PackageType` at FF's one value; a function's IAM settings read and left for AWS as `Policies` are; `AWS::SQS::QueuePolicy` and `AWS::S3::BucketPolicy` kept without effect as `AWS::Lambda::Permission` is; `AWS::Partition`, `aws`; the names SAM makes of a function, its URL, its alias and its versions (`${Function.Alias}` in a `Fn::Sub` too). **`parameters.rs`:** `--parameter-overrides` as the SAM CLI reads it (`Key=Value` and `ParameterKey=Key,ParameterValue=Value`, apart by spaces, double quotes and backslashes as it takes them), else `samconfig.toml`'s `[default.deploy.parameters]`, then `global`, a string or a list; each parameter's value given, the last for a name, else its Default, as `Ref` gives it (a Number's as text, a list's items trimmed); what CloudFormation checks of it: its type, `AllowedValues`, lengths and bounds (`AllowedPattern` left to AWS, `ff` having no regular expressions); a name given that the template hasn't, which `sam deploy` passes over. **`resolve.rs`:** the Globals merged into each function as SAM merges them (a mapping by key, a list the Globals' first, any other value, an intrinsic's included, the function's), and `Ref`, `Fn::GetAtt`, `Fn::Sub` and `Fn::Join` resolved from what is known, what isn't left with its known parts resolved, for the deploy's later passes. **The reader:** a value is named at its key's line, else at the value holding it, a short tag's long form at its tag;
