| T-01 The nightly run made green, and a red one seen | 🔄 In progress | Part 1: `dd8abc7`<br>Part 2: `2ff5fdf`<br>Part 3: `e6f5fd3`, the failed jobs' lines: `b9b9a4f`<br>Part 4: `630ba9f`<br>Part 5: `61ccc4c`, its three misplaced redirects: `3a6f06a`<br>Part 6: `ee7ec7d`<br>Part 7: `a3bc17b`<br>Part 8: `03e2186`<br>Part 9: `0523b6f`, the suites' partition loops: `91fe4ef`<br>L3's log flood, a line in the next second: `c4e0f4e`<br>L3's lines of a call in the store's order: this commit | • **Part 1, the platform's suite on a fresh machine:** it failed on GitHub every night at `cp: cannot create regular file 'hack/.kube/ff-platform-e2e.kubeconfig'`: `hack/e2e-platform.sh` copies its cluster's kubeconfig where `gvisor-up.sh` reads it, and only scripts a fresh runner never ran made that directory. `hack/k8s/env.sh`, which names it for every script, now makes it, owner-only since it holds the clusters' credentials; the four copies of that `mkdir` went.<br>• **Part 2, the L3 case that failed on 2026-10-29** (`log-pipeline.it.spec.ts`, a function printing 30,000 lines): two calls to two functions in two namespaces failed at the same instant with the cluster API's `503 error trying to reach service: EOF`, right after the previous case's polling had left both of the API's pooled connections to them idle. **The cause:** Node.js closes an idle keep-alive connection after 5 seconds, while the API's service proxy, in Go, keeps its own 90 seconds and doesn't read Node's `Keep-Alive: timeout=5` hint; a call it sends on a connection the runtime is closing fails, and a POST is never retried. Measured with Node.js 24.22: at the default, a second request on a connection idle for 6 s is never answered; at 130 s, one idle for 65 s, past Node's 60 s headers timeout, is. The Python runtime answers HTTP/1.0 and reuses no connection.<br>• **Fixed where it is, not retried:** the Node.js runtime keeps an idle connection 130 s; its L1 calls on a connection left idle 6 s, and fails without the change (`Keep-Alive: timeout=5`).<br>• **The same race at the edge, found on the way:** Caddy's reverse proxy keeps idle connections to the backend 2 minutes, and each of the backend's six servers closed them at 5 s: rare 502s on POSTs in production. One helper, `BE/src/common/http-server.ts`, now listens and closes for all six, where each had its own copy (the API's server through Nest): idle connections kept 130 s, and on close each connection closed as it goes idle, since a request still under way at close would otherwise hold the close for the whole keep-alive (measured: still open after 5 s); its L1 fails without the sweep.<br>• **Tests:** the runtime's L1 57 (was 56); the backend's L1 154 suites / 2,192 tests (was 153 / 2,188), `tsc` and ESLint clean.<br>• **Seen, not changed:** the backend installs no SIGTERM handler (no Nest shutdown hooks), so a rollout stops it at once, its calls in flight marked `LOST`, as L4 checks; graceful shutdown is a task of its own.<br>• **Part 3, a red night seen:** `hack/lib/nightly-issue.sh`, run by the workflow's new `nightly-report` job after a scheduled run, or one started by hand with `jobs=nightly`: a failed or cancelled job opens one issue, "The nightly run is red", with the run's page and the failed jobs' `FAILED:` and `FAIL` lines, or comments on the one already open; a green run closes it. That job alone is allowed `issues: write` and `actions: read`. `hack/test-nightly-issue.sh`, 11 checks against a stand-in `gh`, runs in `static`. A run started by hand now names its jobs (`gh workflow run ci.yml -f jobs=platform`), so that one job is proven for its own minutes, not all six's; actionlint clean. **On GitHub's first run in that mode** (41164287966, red: `cli` and `l4`), the issue opened (#1), but without the failed jobs' lines: the run's whole log is served only once the run has ended, and the report's job is part of the run. It reads each failed job's log from the API now, which serves a finished job's at once, as fetching `cli`'s and `l4`'s by hand showed; the test's stand-in answers those two calls, and checks a passed job's log is never read (12 checks).<br>• **Part 4, `master`'s push red on a newer Rust:** step 4's merge (`8da3de4`) failed in `static` on 2026-10-31, at a lint Rust 1.99.1's clippy has and 1.91.0's hasn't (`chunks_exact_to_as_chunks`, twice in `cli/src/md5.rs`): the runner ships 1.99.1, and `hack/install-tools.sh` took any Rust at least 1.91.0. C-08 had left out a `rust-toolchain.toml`, so that a laptop whose `stable` is 1.91.0 needn't download it again under its number; a push failed by a Rust release that changed none of our code costs more, and the decision log's 67 puts the pushes on pinned tools and tries the newest at night. So `cli/rust-toolchain.toml` names 1.91.0, which rustup uses in `cli/` on a laptop and on GitHub alike; `hack/install-tools.sh` installs it through rustup (without rustup, a Rust at least that recent is kept, as before), and `hack/build-cli.sh`'s image takes its version from there: one place, where there were three. `RUSTUP_TOOLCHAIN=stable` tries the newest, for T-09's job. The code takes clippy's suggestion anyway (`as_chunks`, stable since 1.88, which leaves no indexing), checked with both toolchains. Installing the pinned one on this laptop, whose `stable` is 1.91.0, took 65 s, once.<br>• **Part 5, the images' test red on 2 nights of 4,** found on 2026-10-31 while reading the runs before T-05: on 2026-10-26 and 10-28 `cluster` failed at `its metrics, on their own port`, a second after the backend was alive and ready. **The cause:** the check was `curl … | grep -q`, and the suites run under `pipefail`: `grep -q` exits at its first match, near the top of the 13 KB answer, and curl, still writing the rest, fails (23), so that a match reads as none whenever the answer comes in more than one piece, as on a busy runner. Here, where it comes in one, eight runs and ten scrapes passed; shown alone, a writer still writing after the match fails it 20 times in 20, and two small writes 9 in 20. **Fixed everywhere, not only there:** the 102 pipes into `grep -q` in 18 scripts read with `grep … >/dev/null` now, which reads everything: the suites, `hack/install-tools.sh` (`k3d version` and `kubectl version` print line by line: a cut-off pipe read as "not installed"), `deploy/k8s/platform/deploy.sh` and the dev server's script; the metrics check reads a file and names the answer's status; `static` refuses a new pipe into `grep -q` (`no_pipe_into_grep_q`, which catches a planted one). `| head` cuts a writer short the same way, but only an assignment or a condition reads its status, and each of those reads one small write (`docker port`, a buffered `grep -o`): they stay. **Three of the 102 conversions were wrong,** found by `feat/step-5`'s first run on GitHub: where the pattern's `$( … )` held quotes of its own, the redirect went inside it (`--arg id " >/dev/null$meter_id"`), so L4's "the CSV has each function's cost" failed (424 checks passed, 1 failed, every check run: T-02 at work), and two checks of the `ff` suite would have; `bash -n` can't see that. Fixed, and the other 98 checked by a scanner of the shell's quoting: each redirect at its `grep`'s own level.<br>• **Part 6, the `ff` suite's database, found on `feat/step-5`'s first run on GitHub** (41164287966, `cli` red after 11 minutes): `hack/k8s/app-database.sh up` said its PostgreSQL didn't start, 2 s into a wait of 90. **The cause, measured on a fresh `postgres:15-alpine`:** its image's first start runs a server on its socket alone while it initializes, then restarts it; `pg_isready` on the socket said ready at 7.9 s, not ready from 8.1 s, and ready for good at 9.6 s, while over TCP it said ready once only, at 9.4 s, for the server that stays. The wait broke at the first answer and the check after it fell in the gap. **Fixed everywhere:** the nine readiness checks on a socket, in 8 files (the pipeline's database stage, the platform's and the images' suites, the backup drill, the app database, both compose files' health checks, a spike) ask over TCP (`-h 127.0.0.1`), as FF's own database and cache images already did; `static` refuses a check on the socket (`postgres_waited_over_tcp`, which catches a planted one); the app database comes up in 14 s on a first start now, and answers. The run's `failure-cli` artifact held what T-03 keeps (16 MB: pods, events, the backend's and the node's logs), its first use on GitHub; the app database's own log wasn't among them, which the measurement above made up for.<br>• **Part 7, `master`'s push red in `unit` after step 5's merge** (`5c750a3`, run 41172834259, on 2026-10-31): part 2's L1 test of the close received nothing from the server, so the cluster's five jobs never ran. **Two races, both in the test, not in the helper:** the test closed the server after waiting 100 ms for it to have the request, and a connection whose request hasn't arrived is idle, so closing it at once is right, as on a busy runner; and it read what the client had received as soon as the server's close was told, while the client, in the same process, reads only on its next turn: the sweep can close the connection 2 ms after the answer is sent, before the client has read it. Shown outside Jest: 10 runs of 20 read nothing, none once the client's own close is awaited; waiting for the request alone made it worse (9 of 15 failed), since the sweep then fell right after the answer. The test waits for the server to have the request, then for the server's close and the client's: 25 runs in a row passed. A client in another process, as Caddy is, reads an answer that was sent before the close.<br>• **Part 8, L5 red in `invoices` on a run at 23:16 UTC on 31 October** (run 41251175490, X-03's first part): "last month's drafts" got October's where September's were expected, with a second payment. The backend takes last month in Tunisia (`InvoicesService.lastMonth`), where it was 00:16 on 1 November; the suite took last month, and the bank statement's day of its transfers, in UTC, so its transfer of the day fell in October. The suite dates both in Tunisia now. And the backend refused a transfer dated after today as UTC has it, so a bank statement of Tunisia's today in the hour before UTC's midnight, which the suite now sends: it takes Tunisia's today (`receivedOn must be a day that has come in Tunisia`), and so does the admin app's transfer form, its default and its latest day, where it took UTC's. L2 a case of that hour, the admin app one of the day; L5 at 1,044 checks as before<br>• **Part 9, a run across 31 October's end** (run 41254052425, at 23:3x UTC, X-03's second part): L5 red in four checks, L4 in nine, the ff suite in one. **L5's were part 8's:** it expected Tunisia's day where the backend wrote UTC's, in "the transfer was recorded on" and "the certificate was added on", which named yesterday to an operator in Tunis an hour after midnight; and the overview counted the invoices issued in UTC's month, where the one issued at 00:56 in Tunisia was dated 1 November. The backend names those days in Tunisia now, and the overview counts the invoices of Tunisia's month, which it names (`invoices.month`, beside the UTC `month` of its money), as the admin app's Overview now says. **L4's and the ff suite's** came after midnight UTC: "this month" was November, and the calls checked were October's. A suite whose checks count this month's usage now waits for the month's end when it comes within its own run, about (`e2e_within_one_month`: L4 15 minutes, the ff suite 12, L5 8), so a run by hand in a month's last minutes waits as long as it runs at most, within its job's time limit; the nightly, at 02:17 UTC, never does. L2 the duplicate's message at 23:30 UTC naming Tunisia's day, the overview at that hour of 30 November counting December's invoices; the admin app checks the invoices' fields against the API's.<br>• **Found by L2 on 1 November, three tests dated in October:** `invoices`, `buckets` and the month's usage wrote rows of October 2026 (a call, a bucket's measures, a queue's requests) into tables partitioned by month, and the backend makes this month's partition and the next two: in October that was there, from 1 November nothing made it (`no partition of relation "usage_records" found for row`). One function now makes every metering table's partition of the months given (`monthPartitions`), the backend's months ahead among them, and those three suites, and the overview's, make the months they write in; the other suites' own loops are the next commit's. **And the overview's minute-long cache** served the overview it kept to any earlier moment too, so the new test's, at 30 November, was the next test's at 20 November: it serves it in the minute after it was worked out now, not before.<br>• L1 158 suites / 2,252 tests as before; L2 54 suites / 577 tests (576 before), `migration:check` over 62 migrations; the admin app 18 files / 164 tests, the console 422 and `ff` 336 in 22 binaries as before.<br>• **Refactored after it:** the ten other L2 suites that made their months' partitions with a loop of their own, each over the tables it wrote (`capacity`, the estimate at AWS's prices, pricing, the admin's prices, monitoring, the hourly rating, alarms, customers' metrics, the admin's accounts, caches' samples), make them with `monthPartitions`, every metering table's, so a table a test comes to write is never the one its loop left out; three that make the month of a call so many seconds ago, in SQL, stay. L2 54 suites / 577 tests, as before<br>• **L3's log flood, a line in the next second** (2026-11-03, found by the L3 run that tested `2f455bd`, which stored 2,001 lines of a flood in a second where the limit keeps 2,000): Fluent Bit gave ff.lua each line's time as a number, which at today's times is a quarter of a microsecond apart from the next it can say, so a line in a second's last tenth of a microsecond counted in the next, where VictoriaLogs files it, to the nanosecond, in its own; a flood over the budget then stores one line more in that second, once in a few runs. The filter now gets the time as its seconds and nanoseconds apart (`time_as_table`), and counts each line by the seconds alone, as the store files it; a number, as before, still counts by its whole part. **Tested here:** the filter's tests in Fluent Bit 5.1.3's image, **13** (12 before), the new one at today's times: the second's last nanosecond kept in it, refused over the budget, the next second's first kept, and the tick's notice for that second with its count; the configuration accepted (`--dry-run`); applied to the cluster (`hack/k8s/logging-up.sh`), L3's log pipeline suite **9 tests** passed.<br>• **L3's lines of a call in the store's order** (2026-11-03, found by run 41601316725, which tested `357aa39`: Node.js's call ended with `END`, not `REPORT`): the case sorted the stored lines by their `_time` as strings, and VictoriaLogs gives a time's fraction without its trailing zeros (of 40 lines read, 5 with five digits), so `…01.12345Z`, a microsecond ending in 0, came after `…01.123451Z`, its `Z` above the digits: a call's `END` after its `REPORT` when the two share the digits before. The case now asks the store for them in order, `sort by (_time, _msg)`, as the backend reads a function's log. **Tested here:** L3's log pipeline suite, **9 tests**, passed. |
| T-02 Every failing check reported, not only the first | ✅ Done | `841a8a6`<br>Part 4: `16af3d9` | • **One library, `hack/lib/checks.sh`, where ten scripts had a copy of `check` each** (L4, L5 and the `ff` suite through `hack/lib/e2e-stack.sh`; the platform's suite, its images', its manifests', RBAC's, monitoring's, the backup drill, the docs', npm audit's and the nightly issue's tests): a failed check is recorded with its section and its time, and the suite goes on; `fail` still ends the run, for what nothing after it can pass without. At exit the suite's own cleanup runs first (`on_exit`, the last added first), then every failure is listed, last in the output, a later one in the same section marked as after the one above; a run that stopped early says where: at `fail`, or at a command that failed under `set -e`, with its line. Failures go to a file, not a variable, so that a check in a subshell or a pipeline's loop is never lost, each on one line whatever its value held. `FF_FAIL_FAST=1` stops at the first, as before. The suites' `echo "== …"` lines are `section` calls, and their last lines `checks_done`, which fails the run on any failure.<br>• **`hack/test-checks.sh`**, 19 assertions of its own (it can't be its own oracle): failures planted in two sections both listed and the checks after them run, a multi-line value one failure (it counted two before the fix), a subshell's and a loop's failures kept, `fail` and a failed command stopping the run and saying where, fail-fast, the cleanup's order, the Markdown; in `static`.<br>• **`hack/ci.sh`'s report:** each step's result and seconds, then the failed checks, in `hack/.ci/report.md`, GitHub's job summary when there; one exit path with its cleanups, where `stage_db` set and cleared the EXIT trap itself.<br>• **The backup drill** checks first, records after: a drill with a failed check is never recorded as passed.<br>• **Found on the way:** RBAC's suite failed 3 checks when run right after itself, its namespaces from the run before still terminating; it waits for them now (two runs in a row: 44 and 44).<br>• **Counts unchanged:** the platform's manifests 32 before and after; RBAC 44; the docs' checker 6, npm audit's 12; L4 425, as step 4 left it, run here on the new library in 1,087 s. L5, the `ff` suite and the images run on GitHub's first split run (T-04). Not run: monitoring's suite, which needs the operator's monitoring installed and isn't in CI; it only moved onto the library.<br>• **Part 4, the pipeline's steps too** (added on 2026-10-31, while putting the images' test after the scans in one job, T-05): `hack/ci.sh` stopped at its first failed step, so that one ESLint error hid clippy's, and one image's vulnerability the nine other images' scans. A failed step is named now and the next one runs; `prepare`, for what the steps after it need (`npm ci`, Rust, the runtime images, the dev cluster, the platform's tools), still stops the run, and the report's headline says where. `FF_FAIL_FAST=1` stops at the first failure, as in the suites. The steps, their cleanups and the report moved into `hack/lib/ci-steps.sh`, so that they are tested: `hack/test-ci-steps.sh`, 15 assertions, in `static`; each of two broken copies of the library fails it (one stopping at every failure: the step between two failed ones didn't run; one whose end forgot the failures: a red run passed).<br>• **Its done-when, met by T-06's sections (2026-10-31):** in a copy of L4 with three failures planted, run with `--only queues,notifications`, the failed checks of `buckets` and `queues` were reported with their sections' 62 and 46 others, `mappings` stopped at its planted `fail`, `notifications`, which needs it, was listed as not run, and every other section counted what it counts in a whole run. |
| T-03 What a failed run leaves behind | ✅ Done | `5157128`<br>Part 4: `1e3a3f0`<br>Part 5, a stack's start that fails says why: `71a3e3a` | • **`hack/lib/evidence.sh`:** a failed suite's cleanup keeps, before its namespaces and containers go, in `hack/.ci/failure/<suite>/`: every pod and the recent events (probes failing, containers restarted or killed), the run's namespaces' pods described with each one's log and the log before a restart, the k3d node's own log since the run started (the kubelet's and k3s's lines, which keep what a deleted namespace's events lose), the run's containers' and compose services' logs, and the suite's own logs written during the run, the backend's whole. It never fails a run that failed already. Wired into L4, L5 and the `ff` suite (through `hack/lib/e2e-stack.sh`), the platform's suite, L3 (`hack/k8s/test-it.sh`, which keeps its exit status) and the images' suite.<br>• **On GitHub,** each job uploads `hack/.ci/failure/` and `hack/.ci/report.md` as `failure-<job>` when it fails, kept 7 days; `hack/ci.sh` empties the directory at each run's start and says where it is at the end.<br>• **Proven by planted failures:** L4 with its backend on port 1, which it can't listen on: the list names the failure and says the run stopped early at it, and 8 MB were left behind (pods, events, compose, `ff-system` and `ff-logging`, the node's log, the logs); L3 on a path that matches no spec: exit 1 kept, 1.4 MB left behind. That run showed a flaw, fixed: `hack/.e2e/` outlives its runs, and an older run's logs came along; only those written during this run are kept now.<br>• **`hack/test-evidence.sh`,** 9 checks against the dev cluster (what is kept, an older run's log left out, a cluster it can't reach), in the cluster stage. The dev guide's "When a run fails" says how to read it all and how to run what a GitHub runner runs, from a fresh worktree.<br>• **Part 4, an upload that hung took its job's log with it** (`master`'s push of 2026-10-31, run 41174119242): `security`'s suites failed after 11 minutes, very likely at the same load guard as the cluster jobs' (T-04's part 4: `hack/test-images.sh` asks it after the images' builds and scans), then the upload of `failure-security` hung until the job's own limit, 39 minutes later; GitHub kept no log of the job, nor its failure's files. Each of the workflow's nine uploads now has 5 minutes, so that one which hangs fails alone and the job ends with its log.<br>• **Part 5, a stack's start that fails says why** (found on 2026-10-31, `hack/ci.sh static db l5` on a laptop): L5 stopped 21 s in, "compose dependencies did not become healthy", every section not run, its Mailpit created and never started. The suites threw compose's own words away; Docker's log had them: `failed to bind host port 127.0.0.1:58026/tcp: address already in use`, the port free again a moment later. **The cause:** every fixed port of the suites' stacks, L4's 55432 and 59003, L5's five, the `ff` suite's 55436 and 59007, was in Linux's range for outgoing connections, 32768 to 60999 here and on GitHub's runners, where a connection of the machine's takes a port for itself for a while, and a server can't be published on it then. **Fixed:** the ports are under that range, L4's 25432 and 29003, L5's 25433, 29004, 28180, 21026 and 28026, the `ff` suite's 25436 and 29007, their variables moving them as before; and each of the stacks' four starts, through `e2e_up`, ends with compose's last words, as Docker's above, where it said only that a service "did not become healthy". The dev guide says the ports. **Checked:** `hack/ci.sh db l5`, L5's stack on its new ports and its 1,044 checks passed after `db`'s run; GitHub's run of the three commits (41237963321, `71a3e3a`), every job green, L4's and the `ff` suite's stacks on their new ports too. |
| T-04 The cluster job split into five jobs that run at once | 🔄 In progress: `master`'s first green push answered in 26 min 15 s; under 25 minutes is T-07's | Parts 1 and 2: `a30bd91`<br>Part 3: `138f771`<br>Part 4: `ca1cfe9`<br>`master`'s push measured: `53bd876`<br>Part 5, a stage's settings kept to it: `804cc9c` | • **Part 1, five stages in `hack/ci.sh`:** `l3` (L3, the platform's manifests, and the evidence's own test), `l4`, `l5`, `cli` (the `ff` suite) and `images`; each makes or reuses the dev cluster and builds the runtime images, once a run whatever the stages asked for, so that a stage runs alone; `cluster` runs the five in a row, as a laptop does. Traced with a stand-in for `step`: `cluster` runs each of its nine steps once, `l4` alone its three.<br>• **Part 2, one job on five runners:** the workflow's `cluster` job is a matrix of the five stages, one definition where the plan said five copies: gated on `static`, `unit` and `db` as before, `fail-fast: false` so that every stage's failures are reported, each stage with its time limit (`l4` and `cli` 30 minutes, the others 20), its check named by the stage (`l4`, not `cluster (l4, 30)`) and its artifact `failure-<stage>`. A run started by hand names stages (`-f jobs=l4,cli`), or `cluster` for the five; a stage it doesn't name skips its steps, and still costs a minute, since GitHub rounds each job up to a whole one. The disk's clean-up prints the free space before and after, to see what of its 91 s is needed (T-05).<br>• **Part 3, measured** on the branch's runs of 2026-10-31, the first with every cache cold and the second green, against `master`'s last green push of 2026-10-24: **a push is answered in 24 min 49 s, against 52 min 10 s:** the gate's 5 min 26 s (`db` the slowest), then `l4`'s 19 min 20 s. A failure is known at 12 min 36 s in `l3`, 13 min 18 s in the images' test, 13 min 42 s in `l5`, 23 min 8 s in the `ff` suite and 24 min 49 s in `l4`, where each was known when the one cluster job ended, at up to 52 minutes. GitHub bills a push 80 and 82 minutes, against 68: the cluster jobs' setups, as foreseen. Of each cluster job's setup, 40 to 110 s went to making room on the disk (`df` printed 13 GB free, 31 after); `hack/check-resources.sh` asks 6 before each suite, so the room is made in the background now, while the setup and the build go on, by `.github/actions/setup` (`room: true`), where three jobs had the same line.<br>• **Part 4, three of the five cluster jobs refused on `master`'s push** (run 41174119242, 2026-10-31): `l3`, `l5` and `cli` stopped within 2 s of their suite, at `hack/check-resources.sh`, the runner's load at 4.13 to 4.50 against its limit of 4 (2 CPUs × 2). **The cause, part 3's own:** the disk's clean-up runs in the background now, and deleting its 18 GB keeps the load up while the cluster starts and the images go in: at each suite's start the load read 1.70 to 2.87 when the clean-up ran first (run 41166488513), 3.43 with it in the background on the branch's run, and 4.13 to 4.50 here. A runner runs one job alone, its load that job's own steps: waiting frees nothing, and refusing fails a run for no fault in the code. **Fixed:** `MAX_LOAD_FACTOR=off` reports the load without refusing on it, the disk and the memory still checked, and the workflow sets it for every job; a laptop keeps its limit. `hack/test-check-resources.sh`, 4 checks in `static`, fails without the change.<br>• **`master`'s first green push, measured** (run 41178141641, `1e3a3f0`, 2026-10-31): every suite green and the counts unchanged (L1 2,192, L2 561, L3 33 with 3 skipped, L4 425, L5 1,036, the `ff` suite 223, the images 29, the platform's manifests 32), 81 minutes billed. **Answered in 26 min 15 s, not under 25:** `changes` 11 s, then the gate's 5 min 35 s (`db`'s 322 s the slowest), then `l4`'s 20 min 26 s, its suite 18 min 41 s of it after 89 s of setup; the branch's runs had 24 min 49 s, and a run varies by a minute, `l4` the most. The other jobs ended by 23 minutes, the `ff` suite last of them. The minutes to take off are the suites' own: T-07, their waits overlapped, aims at a third off L4's and the `ff` suite's, which would answer a push in about 20 minutes.<br>• **On the public runners, said so (2026-10-31):** with the repository public for a while (the decision log's 68), `master`'s push after the fast-forward to `28660b0` answered in 22 min 35 s, `l4`'s 18 min 28 s the last; their 4 CPUs, where the private runners have 2, say nothing of the 25 minutes, which stay the private runners'. With T-07 and T-08 in, `master`'s push after the fast-forward to `c7cc004` (run 41226854762, public runners still) answered in 18 min 31 s, every suite green: the gate in 4 min 46 s (`db`'s 276 s the slowest), then `l4`'s 13 min 42 s the last, the `ff` suite's 9 min 33 s, `l3`'s 6 min 44 s and `l5`'s 5 min 3 s. **L3's one check that went red on GitHub's runners:** a call's lines stored within 5 s, the suite's first call through a pipeline just started, took 9.5 s at `c77d2d2` and 7.9 s at `f677467`, under 5 s on a laptop; it has the live tail's 15 s now, which reads the same store, and the dev guide says so.<br>• **Part 5, a stage's settings kept to it** (found on 2026-10-31, `hack/ci.sh unit db static l5` on a laptop): L5 failed 46 checks, every database and cache it made refused with 503, its backends reaching the customers' database server on port 32842 and the cache server's caches on 54206 to 54215. **The cause:** `db`'s stage exported its own servers' settings, ports of this machine's among them, and the stages after it in the same run kept them; L4's, L5's and the `ff` suite's backends are given their servers' hosts but not their ports, and took these. On GitHub each stage is a job of its own, so a run of several stages on one machine alone met it. **Fixed:** `db`'s settings go to its two steps alone, through `env`, and the suites' backends are given their servers' ports too, `FF_DATA_DB_PORT=5432` and `FF_CACHE_PORTS=6400-6999`, as the suites' containers listen, so that neither the environment nor a developer's `BE/.env` can change them. **Checked:** `hack/ci.sh static db l5` in one run, `db` before `l5` as when it failed: L5's 1,044 checks passed, L2's 53 suites / 571 tests as before. |
| T-05 Nothing built twice, nothing downloaded twice | 🔄 In progress: measured; the `ff` suite's cache to see on a run after `master`'s first | Parts 1 and 2: `26cda3b`<br>Part 3: `138f771` | • **Measured first, on the last green run's logs (2026-10-24):** the console's image takes 143 s to build and the edge's 125 s, in `security` and again in `cluster` (the images' test, the manifests'); clippy 38 s and `cargo test` 64 s, mostly compiling the crates, and the `ff` suite's release build 128 s; `npm ci` 29 s a job, of which the console's and the admin app's 12 s in jobs that don't use them; Trivy's database about 10 s to fetch; kubectl and k3d 1 s.<br>• **Part 1, nothing downloaded that isn't used, nor twice:** `.github/actions/setup`, each job's setup, where seven jobs had their own `setup-node`: Node.js 24.21.0 with npm's cache over the lockfiles the job installs from, and for the three jobs that build `ff` (`static`, `unit`, `cli`) Cargo's registry and `cli/target`, each its own, keyed by the toolchain and `Cargo.lock`, a changed lockfile starting from the last build; `CARGO_INCREMENTAL=0` and line tables only, which keep that cache small. `deps` installs what the stages after it use (`stage_uses`): the console's and the admin app's packages for `static` and `unit` alone, the backend's where it runs here, Rust for the three that build `ff`, nothing for `security` (npm audit reads the lockfile: the same report as with `node_modules`, checked) and `platform`; `hack/ci.sh deps` alone, everything. Not cached, measured not worth it: Trivy's database (10 s to fetch, about 700 MB to keep), kubectl and k3d (1 s).<br>• **Part 2, each image built once:** `images` runs in the `security` job, after the scans, on Docker's build cache, which keeps every layer they built: the same build arguments, so neither the console's build nor the edge's runs again; the manifests' test moved from `l3` to `images` for the edge's image; `hack/ci.sh` names every failed step now (T-02's part 4), so a vulnerability found doesn't keep the images' test from running. The cluster's matrix is `l3`, `l4`, `l5` and `cli`. Docker's cache isn't kept between runs: the default builder can't export it to GitHub's cache, and the images' layers would push the rest out of its 10 GB.<br>• **Part 3, measured** on the same runs: warm, **`static` took 109 s, against 177** (`npm ci` in the backend 8 s against 16, clippy 8 against 42), **and `unit` 218, against 242** (`cargo test`'s build 18 s against 52): a change of `Cargo.lock` or of the toolchain costs its first run 1 min 32 s, once; a Dockerfile's change costs nothing more, the images being built afresh each run. The `ff` suite's release build found no cache on either run, since a job saves its cache only when it passes and the first run's `cli` failed: a run after `master`'s first measures it, a branch's caches being its own and `master`'s every branch's, so that the merge's push starts cold. **The minutes a push costs didn't move,** 80 cold and 82 warm: the cluster jobs vary by a minute from run to run, the disk's room the most of it (T-04's part 3, now in the background). The aim of about 65 is the suites' own time, T-07's and T-08's. **The disk's room in the background,** on the branch's run by hand of 2026-10-31: `security` took 668 s, against 794, and `l3` 335, against 427.<br>• **The `ff` suite's build from `master`'s cache (2026-10-31, run 41198067154):** the cargo cache that `master`'s first run saved was restored (`Cache hit for: cargo-cli-…`), and the section that builds `ff` took 103 s, against 188 s on `master`'s first, cold run; but on the public runners, 4 CPUs against 2 (the decision log's 68), so the cache's own share is to be measured on a private run, when the repository is private again. On `master`'s push at `c7cc004`, the cache key hashing `cli/Cargo.toml` too since the suite's own profile (T-07's part 2), the cache came from the key before, a prefix's match, and was saved under the new one; the section that builds `ff` and the backend took 91 s, a second over its budget, which the run flagged and nothing failed for. |
| T-06 A suite's sections run alone, and against a stack already up | ✅ Done | Parts 1 and 2, L4: `f2af1df`<br>Parts 1 and 2, L5 and the `ff` suite: `77a2b6e`<br>Part 3: `797bc3e` | • **The runner, `hack/lib/sections.sh`:** a suite's section is a function, `section_<id>`, its `section "…"` title its first line, registered after it with the sections it needs (`section_def mappings section_mappings queues`). `sections_run` runs them in order, each in a subshell of its own: a command that fails under `set -e`, or `fail`, ends that section alone, recorded at its line; each section that needs it is listed as not run, and the others run; `FF_FAIL_FAST=1` ends the run at the first. What a section leaves reaches the sections after it as in one shell: its new and changed values (`printf %q`), its arrays and values with attributes (`declare -p`, made global), the functions it defined; the shell's own variables and the environment's stay out. `--list` prints each section's id, its seconds on the last run, its title and what it needs; `--only a,b` runs those after what they need, transitively; `--from x` that one and every one after it, with what they need.<br>• **Only what changed is passed on:** passing every value after each section took 849 ms a section with L4's values, an answer of 2 MB among them, about 28 s a run; passing the new and changed ones, 199 ms, about 7 s. The unchanged are compared, not fingerprinted, which could keep a stale value.<br>• **L4 in 33 sections:** the stack's setup a section of its own; the helpers more than one section calls (a function's code, `zip_of`, `upload`, `env_q`, `sql`, `q`, the AWS CLI's command) moved before the first, so that a failure in one is reported at the suite's own line. The AWS CLI's command and an account of no one's are set before the first section now, and the S3 endpoint beside its port, in `backend`; `queues` still needs `buckets`, for the other account's key and the clients `buckets` installs, and splitting those out, so that `--only queues` skips `buckets`' 186 s, is part 3's. `wait` on a backend another section started became `e2e_gone`, which polls (`hack/lib/e2e-stack.sh`): such a process is no child of the shell after it. Each section's needs were read off the variables it and its helpers read, and the helpers it calls, by a script, then by hand; what is shared only through the backend's data can't be read so, which the runs below test.<br>• **Proven:** the whole run, 425 checks passed, as `master`'s last on GitHub (1,432 s on this machine); `--only queues` while it still needed `buckets`, 112 checks, each section counting what it counts in the whole run, in 6 min 27 s; with three failures planted (T-02's done-when, its row), every other section counting as in the whole run; `--only queues,notifications,mail`, nine sections, 165 checks in 6 min 49 s, each section counting what it counts in the whole run. A run of `queues` without `buckets` failed, as it must: the other account's key and the clients' helpers come from `buckets`, which the needs' script had missed, a helper's closing brace taken for its section's end; mended, it finds them.<br>• **L5 and the `ff` suite, in 28 and 19 sections,** converted as L4 was, the helpers more than one section calls before the first (L5's eighteen, the `ff` suite's `ff`, `out`, `err` and `code`), each need read off what a section and its helpers read, by the script mended above. Whole, they counted 1,036 and 223 checks, as `master`'s last runs on GitHub, in 5 min 55 s and 16 min 5 s on this machine; L4 whole at `f2af1df`, its sections as they stand, counted 425 on GitHub (run 41198400876). Run alone, every section counting what it counts in the whole run: L5's `--only overview,price_books`, the operator's side after the stack, the backends and `admin`, 17 checks in 132 s; the `ff` suite's `--only account`, whose usage and metrics count the account's calls, after `backend` and `failures` alone, 54 checks in 290 s; and its `--only queues`, after the stack, `backend` and `buckets`, 68 checks in 9 min 15 s. `--list` sizes its first column to the suite's longest id.<br>• **Part 3, the stack kept and reused (`hack/lib/e2e-stack.sh`):** `--keep` leaves the compose project and the run's backends, namespaces and containers up, written for the next run in the suite's work directory (`kept.sh`); `--reuse` runs against them: what the kept run made beside its compose project goes first, FF's database is made new, and the services that hold what a run left are made anew with their volumes (the customers' database server, the cache, the mail server and its stand-in receiver, Mailpit), while FF's PostgreSQL and S3 servers, Keycloak, whose users each run makes its own, and the cluster stay. A run without `--reuse` removes a kept stack whole first. `--keep` is refused on GitHub, and an unknown option, or `--reuse` with nothing kept, is refused, each with exit 2 and nothing touched (`checks_quit` in `hack/lib/checks.sh`, the runner's `sections_quit` before). The cleanup's removal is one helper now, `e2e_remove`, for the run's own and for a kept run's; the three suites read their options through one, `e2e_options`. **For every run, kept or not:** the runtime images are imported into the cluster only when its node lacks these very ones, by their IDs, as Docker and containerd both give them (an image built again, or one the kubelet removed for room, is imported again): 16 s off; the backend is built only when what it is built from changed (each file's name, time and size, and the build's own time, kept in the build, which nest empties when it builds): 12 s off. **And `queues` no longer waits on `buckets`:** a section of its own, `keys`, makes the other account's key and installs AWS's clients, which `queues`, `notifications`, `sites` and `mail` took from `buckets`; the account's first key stays in `buckets`, whose last line deletes it, as `queues` makes two of its own under the limit of two, which a run of `queues` alone found. **Measured on this machine:** `hack/e2e.sh --only queues` in 107 s on a stack of its own and 104 s reused, 50 checks each, where it took 6 min 27 s while it still waited on `buckets`; L5's `--only overview,price_books` 81 s, then 66 s reused, 17 checks each; the `ff` suite's `--only account` 280 s, then 186 s, 54 checks each. Each kept run left its compose project, its backends (L5's two) and its record up; each reused one, which kept nothing, left no container, backend or record. Reusing saved 3 s on L4's run, 15 on L5's and 94 on the `ff` suite's: the services that hold a run's data, made anew, take about 30 of the stack's 50 s, and the skips for every run save more. The three suites whole after it all: L4 425 checks, in 1,119 s against 1,432 before, its stack 43 s against 74 and its backend 10 against 150; L5 1,036, in 334 s against 355; the `ff` suite 223, in 889 s against 965.<br>• **`hack/test-sections.sh`,** 17 assertions on suites of its own (values, arrays, maps and functions passed on, a value changed passed again, quotes and a newline intact, a stop at its line, `fail`, the cleanup still run, `--only`, `--from`, `--list`, an unknown section refused, `FF_FAIL_FAST`), in `static`; two broken copies of the runner, one passing no changed value and one writing values unquoted, each fail it.<br>• **On the way:** `hack/lib/checks.sh` says what `fail` does in a suite of sections; the dev guide's "A section alone"; the suites' times in getting-started and the dev guide, which said 3 minutes for L4 and 1 for L5, as GitHub measured them: about 20, 7 and 15 minutes for L4, L5 and the `ff` suite. Counts: L4's 425 before and after; `hack/test-sections.sh`'s 17, new. |
| T-07 The minutes the suites spend waiting, overlapped | ✅ Done: on the same runners, L4 32 % off, the `ff` suite 50 %, L5 34 %, their counts unchanged; the private runners' measure is T-04's | Part 1: `eeac5b6`<br>Part 4, L5: `3b126bb`<br>Part 1's fix: `3e2e585`<br>Part 2: `fc3a9d8`<br>Parts 3 and 4: `42af848` | • **Part 1, started early, read late** (2026-10-31): what waits on the product's clock is started in its section and read a few sections on, in a section of its own that needs it, so that its minute passes while the sections between run; each check is the one it was, against a real minute of the product's, and a late section run alone waits as long as its section did. Each wait has its own pair, not one first section and one last: the alarm's state is read while it lasts, FF keeping no history of it. **In L4:** `schedules` right after `scenario`, `schedules_ran` after `caches`, the runs of the schedules' first minute read by their start, since they run every minute after, the schedules once none says its run is running, and the failing one's retry a minute after its failure the real minute now (the worker claiming it within the second), where the section cut it short in FF's database; `async_metrics`, `databases_sampled`, `caches_sampled` and `sites_counted` after `plans` and before `usage_records` kills the backend, whose counts of the minute are in its memory until written, a check's "within a minute" read from the backend's log, which in development has each query with its time (`written_within`); `usage_lost` after `alarms`, moved between them, whose two minutes the reaper's passes in; in `buckets`, the other account's storage and the 4xx alarm started before AWS's clients, whose minute they pass in. **In the `ff` suite:** `schedules_ran` after `packages`, the schedule disabled before its history is read, so that no run starts between two reads; `databases_used` before the small app, which makes the plan's one database; `buckets_used`, `sites_counted` and `queues_counted` after it, the keys revoked where they were, as an account has two at most.<br>• **Measured on this machine:** L4 whole, 425 checks as before, in 796 s against 1,119 (29 % off): the schedule's 80 s to 16 (its two sections), the asynchronous calls' 100 to 33, the databases' 60 to 10, the caches' 61 to 9, the sites' 85 to 14, the usage records' 91 to 39, the buckets' 185 to 139; the alarms' 114 to 131, their two minutes' phase. The `ff` suite, 223 as before, in 699 s against 889 (21 % off): the schedule's 70 to 5, the database's 34 to 31, the buckets' 105 to 10, the site's 55 to 1, the queues' 122 to 12; its first section 156 s against 56, `ff` built again for the new commit, as every commit's first build is (part 2).<br>• **Found on the way:** L4's rating check failed a minute and a half each hour, at 14:32 here: it counted an hour settled 33 minutes after its end, the default cold start's, while the backend that answers it in a whole run, restarted with a 1 s cold start, waits 31 min 31 s; it follows the running backend's cold start now (`be_ready_timeout`). The bucket's metrics check read the API and the counts once each, and a minute's counts written between the two reads would fail it, likelier in the new order: both read again until they agree, a minute at most. `sql_e2e`, `sql`'s copy in `buckets`, gone. Another project's container took L4's PostgreSQL port here during the work (127.0.0.1:55432): `e2e_dependencies` names a port another program holds, and the variable that moves it (`FF_E2E_PG_PORT`), where compose said only that its dependencies were not healthy.<br>• **On GitHub before it,** run 41206929125 at `797bc3e` (2026-10-31, the public runners): every suite green, L4 425 checks in 995 s, L5 1,036 in 354 s, the `ff` suite 223 in 821 s.<br>• **On GitHub after part 1,** run 41214639514 at `eeac5b6` (the public runners, as the run before): every suite green, L4 425 checks in 646 s against 995 (35 % off), the `ff` suite 223 in 595 against 821 (28 % off), and L5, which part 1 left as it was, 1,036 in 312 against 354.<br>• **Part 4, L5** (2026-10-31): its two loops over every route, B's in development mode and account 2's, each made more changes in a minute than beta's 60, and the rate limit held each a minute: `patient` slept 60 s in each, 120 of its 335. The accounts the loops run as are on a plan of the test's own now, `l5-loops`, beta's limits but 10,000 changes a minute, set in FF's database as L4 sets its own, so that the operator's audit log, whose entries a check counts, has none more; `patient` and `patiently` are gone with the waits. L5 whole: 1,036 checks as before, in 333 s against 430, the two loops' sections from 75 s each to 20 and 16; the backend was built again in it (44 s): `static` had built it just before, and a suite builds it again when `BE/dist` isn't the build it recorded (part 2). On the way, the routes are read from the API's document once, with the query parameters each requires (`route_queries`), where `path_for` read the whole document again for each route of the three loops; and the psql command's twelve copies are two helpers, `sql` and `sql_value`, the `overview` and `closing` sections' own among them.<br>• **Found on the way:** gitleaks failed `static` on `eeac5b6`, which moved L4's check of a deleted cache's address, and with it the development cache server's own token (`ff-cache-dev`, which production refuses), listed in `.gitleaksignore` for the commit that wrote it: listed where it moved too. L4's account makes its changes as densely and had none refused in its two runs since part 1; its checks quote beta's name in five refusals, so it stays on beta until one is. What the `ff` suite's first section spent was release's link-time optimizations, made again on every commit since the commit is built in: 126 s here after a commit, with two jobs. The suite builds with a profile of its own now, `e2e` in `cli/Cargo.toml`, release's without them: 40 s after a commit and 114 s from nothing, so that even a build from nothing is the faster; a release (`hack/build-cli.sh`, its static musl binary, which no suite ran before either) keeps them. | T-09 The nightly run: what the day's pushes don't do | ✅ Done: answered on 2026-10-31, (a) (the decision log, 67), with the nightly's advisories kept; `master`'s first nights to measure | Part 1: `19adb79`<br>Part 2: `aa05acf`<br>Part 3: `6d24a5f`<br>Part 4: `cafc4a9`<br>Part 5: `1beb0de` | • **Part 1, the schedule runs `platform` alone:** `static`, `unit`, `db`, `security` and the cluster's four stages skip on the schedule, and on a run started by hand with `jobs=nightly`, which runs what the schedule runs: each ran on the commit when it was pushed, and the nightly added nothing to them but minutes. Measured on the branch's green run of 2026-10-31, the nightly with every job billed 98 minutes, 2,940 a month, where the free 2,000 must also pay for the pushes (82 each); `platform` alone bills 15, and the report 1, about 480 a month. The nightly's report needs `platform` alone now, a job skipped by the run's shape being as green as before (`hack/lib/nightly-issue.sh`). The decision log's 39 says the measured minutes.<br>• **Part 2, a push of documents alone runs `static` alone:** a first job, `changes`, compares the push's commits, or the pull request's, with `hack/lib/changes.sh`; `unit`, `db`, `security` and the cluster's stages need it and skip when only documents changed, about 4 minutes billed where a push costs 82. Documents are `docs/` and `*.md`, but the `ff` command's templates, which it embeds: `hack/test-changes.sh`, in `static`, checks that every file `ff` embeds counts as code, and the rule on commits of its own (documents alone, code, the templates, a new branch without a base). A run started by hand, or `changes` failing, runs every job it names, as before. Gitleaks moved from `security` to `static`, the one job such a push runs, so that a commit of documents is still searched for secrets; `security`'s checkout is shallow now. It reads HEAD's history alone, what a push sends: by default it read every ref, and `static` on this laptop failed on a branch never pushed, its made-up keys listed in `.gitleaksignore` under the commit that later took them.<br>• **Part 3, the nightly's advisories,** found while doing part 1: the recommendation said the nightly added nothing to the jobs a push ran but minutes, and that isn't so for the security scans, since advisories come out every day; the SBOMs of 2026-10-24, scanned again on 2026-10-31, had three new ones (go-jose in Caddy's three images, OpenSSL in two). `hack/ci.sh advisories` scans the security stage's SBOMs again with Trivy (`trivy sbom`, which honours `.trivyignore.yaml`'s paths, checked: the Caddy advisory accepted until 2026-11-02 stays accepted) and runs npm audit on the lockfiles, nothing built, about a second an image; on GitHub, the `advisories` job takes the branch's last SBOMs from the security job's `sbom` artifact (`hack/lib/last-sboms.sh`: the newest GitHub still keeps, not another branch's; `hack/test-last-sboms.sh` against a stand-in `gh`). Proven on the branch's run by hand of 2026-10-31 with `jobs=nightly`: every job a push runs skipped, `changes` too; `platform` green in 844 s, `advisories` in 53, the report in 6.<br>• **Part 4, the tools' job:** each night, `hack/ci.sh tools` takes the latest release of each AWS tool the tests pin (`hack/lib/latest-tools.sh`: Docker Hub's tags for the AWS CLI's image, npm and PyPI for the SDKs, GitHub's releases for OpenTofu and the SAM CLI, the SAM CLI's checksum from its release's notes, HashiCorp's for Terraform and the registry for the provider), runs the Lambda spike's flows with them in a folder of their own, and sets what each sent against `hack/spikes/lambda/recordings/` (`compare.py`, which `hack/test-tools-compare.sh` tests in `static`): red when a step that passed fails, a refusal goes through, an operation is called or no longer called, or a path or a header is signed otherwise; the polls' counts are notes. Rust's stable release runs the `ff` command's clippy and tests, as T-01's part 4 would have caught a night before `master`'s push. Tried here on 2026-10-31 with the AWS CLI 2.39.3, the SDK 3.1161.0, boto3 1.43.123, OpenTofu 1.13.2, Terraform 1.17.1, the provider 6.71.0 and the SAM CLI 1.171.0: every tool as before. Its first try took the CLI's refusal of an update in progress for a change: the pinned run had been let through, each command starting a container that takes about as long as the endpoint's 2 s, so the CLI's flow marks its three such steps `paced`, whose outcome is a note. Proven on the branch's run by hand of 2026-10-31 with `jobs=static,security,l3,tools`: `tools` green in 264 s, the same releases every tool as before, Rust's stable release through clippy and the tests in 65 s; `static` in 176 s with gitleaks and the three new tests.<br>• **Part 5, the newest tools in a directory of their own** (found on 2026-10-31, while starting X-01's part 4): `hack/ci.sh tools` on a laptop had installed the newest OpenTofu (1.13.2), Terraform (1.17.1) and SAM CLI (1.171.0) over the pinned ones in `hack/bin`, which the other stages then ran, against the decision log's 67; on GitHub, a runner a night, it never showed. And the SAM CLI's installer, asked for a version it had installed before, left its link on the newer: `hack/install-tools.sh sam` said 1.169.0 was installed while `sam --version` said 1.171.0. `FF_TOOLS_BIN` and `FF_SAM_DIR` put the newest in `hack/.ci/tools/`, which the stage sets for the Lambda spike's run, the provider's mirror holding each version beside the others as before; the link is now the version asked for, and the install checks that `sam --version` says it. Run by hand on this laptop, the spike passed with the newest tools in 371 s, every tool's requests as recorded, and `hack/bin` kept 1.169.0, 1.13.1 and 1.16.6. |

Set now for every section of the three suites, at half as much again as the longer of a whole run here and on GitHub's public runners, at least 5 s: L4's 41 sections, L5's 28 and the `ff` suite's 24 (1,270, 570 and 985 s in all, against runs of 718, 244 and 610). `hack/test-sections.sh` tests it on a suite of its own (a section past its budget flagged in a whole run and in the report, not when run alone, and listed) and checks that every section of the three suites names one, so that each new section of step 5 does: 23 assertions. L5 whole with its budgets: 1,036 checks as before, in 244 s, none flagged.<br>• **Part 2, the Terraform runs' own job** (2026-11-03, with X-11's part 2): the stage `aws-tools`, `hack/e2e-aws-tools.sh`, and a job of its own, the cluster matrix's sixth, gated like the others (`-f jobs=aws-tools`, or `cluster`), 30 minutes at most, so that `l4` doesn't grow by it; OpenTofu, Terraform and the providers' mirror cached by their versions (the setup action's `aws-tools: terraform`), and `ff`'s build by Cargo's cache, as the `ff` suite's job keeps it. Its sections' budgets at half as much again as a whole run here (181, 189 and 230 s for OpenTofu's, Terraform's and the throttled one), the stack's and the backend's 90 and 120 s (13 and 49 here), since a runner makes the cluster and builds `ff` first; `hack/ci.sh suggest` names the stage for the templates, `ff init`, the suite and the provider's log reader. | T-11 Before a commit: which stages and sections a change needs | ✅ Done | `30f9936` | • **`hack/ci.sh suggest [<commit>]`** (2026-10-31) runs nothing: it reads the working tree's changes against HEAD, the files not added yet among them, or everything since a commit named, and prints the stages and the suites' sections that cover them, as commands in their order: the resource check, then each stage once, a suite whole when a path needs it whole, else its sections together with `--only`, the stages in a row in one `hack/ci.sh` with `FF_CI_JOBS=2`; then each path and what it needs. It is dispatched before anything of a run's, so that a failed run's files and its report stay as they were.<br>• **The map,** `hack/suggest.map`: a line a path pattern, as `case` reads it, and what a change there needs, the first line a path matches its own, the narrower first: the backend a module at a time where its sections are its own (the queues: `unit`, `db`, L4's `queues`, `mappings`, `notifications` and `sites_counted`, L5 whole, which tries every route, and three of the `ff` suite's), the documents `static`, the `ff` command's templates code, as `hack/lib/changes.sh` has them. No line catches everything: a path the map lacks is said, with every stage.<br>• **Its test,** `hack/test-suggest.sh`, in `static`: every file the repository tracks matches a line, every stage and every section a line names exists (each suite's own `--list`), a path takes its first line, and the commands come out in order, whole suites over their sections, a section named twice run once; a planted unknown section and stage, and the catch-all of `hack/` narrowed, each failed it. 10 checks in about a second.<br>• **The rule where it is read:** `hack/ci.sh`'s header and the guide's pipeline section say it: on a machine others share, one heavy stage at a time, the resource check first, `FF_CI_JOBS=2`; `suggest` prints the commands in that order. `static` whole: 218 s, every step green. |
